# Vladimir Putin — 2B (Russian Federation)


## Artifact X

 ## 1. Orientation

### Scope and exposure gate

The period is calendar year 2022 only. Putin plainly passes the conflict-exposure gate: he exercised supreme command over Russian forces, personally announced the 24 February operation, subsequently ordered mobilization, and presided over attempted annexations. The evidence concerns an inherited internationalized conflict that he radically escalated into interstate war—not ordinary domestic policing.

### Authority

Articles 80 and 87 of Russia’s Constitution make the president responsible for basic foreign-policy direction and Supreme Commander-in-Chief. Putin’s public decisions—recognition of the separatist entities, announcement of military operations, September mobilization, and the annexation ceremony—confirm practical as well as formal authority. Operational crimes cannot all be attributed personally, but strategic initiation, escalation, mobilization, annexation, failure to comply with the ICJ order, and system-level remedy fall within his authority.

### Inherited baseline

Russia entered 2022:

- occupying and claiming Crimea since 2014;
- supporting armed formations controlling parts of Donetsk and Luhansk;
- participating in the Minsk, Normandy Four, and Trilateral Contact Group processes;
- facing an unresolved but substantially lower-intensity Donbas conflict;
- confronting a genuine dispute over NATO enlargement and European security.

The OSCE recorded a fragile ceasefire and serious monitoring restrictions. Nevertheless, after the Trilateral Contact Group’s 22 December 2021 recommitment, ceasefire violations fell 60% during the following month. Violations then rose sharply immediately before 24 February, but the OSCE did not assign all incidents to one side and was operating under access constraints. This is an inherited-security problem, not evidence that a nationwide invasion was necessary.

### External shocks and constraints

Relevant constraints included NATO–Russia distrust, Russia’s large force buildup, the deteriorating Donbas ceasefire, Ukraine’s military cooperation with Western states, and failed implementation of Minsk. Yet NATO and the United States delivered written proposals covering dialogue, risk reduction, transparency, and arms control on 26 January. No NATO attack on Russia or internationally accepted genocide finding preceded the invasion.

After 24 February, Ukrainian resistance, Western arms assistance and sanctions, Russian battlefield setbacks, and the absence of mutually accepted security guarantees made settlement harder. These later constraints do not explain the initial decision to invade.

### Information environment

Russian wartime censorship and punishment of dissent make domestic silence weak evidence. Putin’s own speeches are direct evidence of his claims, but not proof those claims were true. International monitoring also had limitations: the OSCE faced movement restrictions; OHCHR and the ICRC lacked full Russian-controlled access; wartime fatality estimates remain incomplete; and the Istanbul negotiations are reconstructed partly from participants and draft texts.

## 2. Compact evidence index

| ID | Fact | Why it matters |
|---|---|---|
| WEB-2B-001 | Presidential command authority | Resolves strategic and remedial attribution. |
| WEB-2B-002 | Fragile but functioning prewar ceasefire | Establishes inherited conflict and a de-escalatory alternative. |
| WEB-2B-003 | NATO’s January written proposals | Shows negotiations and arms-control alternatives remained available. |
| WEB-2B-004 | Putin announced nationwide military operations | Direct attribution for initiation and stated justifications. |
| WEB-2B-005 | UN condemnation and withdrawal demand | Authoritative international assessment of the resort to force. |
| WEB-2B-006 | ICJ provisional order to suspend operations | Shows a binding judicial direction that Russia did not obey. |
| WEB-2B-007 | Attempted annexation of four regions | Demonstrates territorial objectives and escalation beyond Donbas defence. |
| WEB-2B-008 | UCDP conflict lethality | D13 evidence of the scale of interstate war created in 2022. |
| WEB-2B-009 | Verified civilian casualties and abuse patterns | Central evidence on distinction, detention, torture, and proxy conduct. |
| WEB-2B-010 | Summary executions in northern Ukraine | Location-specific evidence of Russian-force killings and precaution failures. |
| WEB-2B-011 | POW access and treatment failures | Evidence concerning Geneva Convention compliance and remedy. |
| WEB-2B-012 | Commission findings and contrary Ukrainian violations | Strong later fact-finding, with important non-Russian violations retained. |
| WEB-2B-013 | Spring negotiations and their unresolved terms | Shows real diplomacy but not a completed peace agreement. |
| WEB-2B-014 | Black Sea Grain Initiative | Material favorable evidence of limited wartime compromise. |
| WEB-2B-015 | Mobilization and military expenditure | Shows escalation after setbacks and D14 spending change. |
| WEB-2B-016 | Year-end displacement and European security deterioration | Measures the regional and humanitarian end state. |
| WEB-2B-017 | ICC child-transfer warrant | Later, limited judicial attribution directly concerning Putin. |

## 3. Disposition by evidence lens

### 2B.1 — Initiation, diplomacy, and alternatives

Strong adverse evidence, with meaningful contrary material. A real security dispute and unstable Donbas conflict existed, but so did the Minsk/OSCE machinery, NATO’s written arms-control proposals, and a recently improved ceasefire. Putin chose a nationwide attack before those avenues were exhausted (WEB-2B-002–006). Spring negotiations and the grain agreement show Russia was capable of diplomacy, but occurred after the invasion (WEB-2B-013–014).

### 2B.2 — War of choice, expansion, coercion, and annexation

Strongly documented. Putin announced operations across Ukraine, continued them after the ICJ suspension order, mobilized additional forces after battlefield reversals, and attempted to annex four Ukrainian regions, including territory Russia did not fully control (WEB-2B-004–008, 015).

### 2B.3 — Defensive necessity versus other objectives

The defensive interpretation has a credible foundation only at the level of general threat perception: NATO enlargement, Western military cooperation with Ukraine, and Donbas insecurity were real Russian concerns. However, the breadth of the invasion, “denazification” and regime-directed rhetoric, attacks far beyond Donbas, and attempted annexations are inconsistent with a narrowly tailored defence of Donetsk and Luhansk. NATO’s January offer of dialogue and risk reduction further limits a necessity claim (WEB-2B-003–007).

### 2B.4 — Civilian protection, proportionality, and prisoners

Extensive adverse institutional findings. OHCHR and the UN Commission documented summary executions, attacks lacking distinction or precautions, torture, sexual violence, arbitrary detention, filtration, and unlawful child transfers. The ICRC lacked access to thousands of POWs. Ukrainian forces also committed documented violations, including torture and likely indiscriminate attacks, but these do not negate the larger Russian patterns or Russia’s obligations as the invading and occupying power (WEB-2B-009–012, 017).

### 2B.5 — Control of proxies, affiliated forces, and clients

Adverse but less complete at the level of individual command orders. OHCHR attributed detention and torture to Russian forces and affiliated armed groups; the ICJ expressly ordered Russia to restrain military or irregular units it directed or supported. The continuation of these patterns, Russia’s integration and later annexation of separatist-held territory, and lack of independent investigation support responsibility at the policy and remedy level. Internal proxy-control records remain unavailable (WEB-2B-006, 009, 011–012).

### 2B.6 — Truthful explanation of threats

Strong evidence of manipulative or unsupported justification. Putin invoked genocide, an imminent existential Western threat, “denazification,” and collective self-defence. The ICJ found Ukraine had a plausible right not to be subjected to operations based on the genocide allegation; Russia presented no substantiated genocide case in that proceeding. The invasion’s territorial reach and subsequent annexations contradicted the narrower collective-defence framing (WEB-2B-004, 006–007).

### 2B.7 — Ceasefires and settlements

Mixed, but adverse overall. Russia participated in Belarus/Istanbul talks and accepted the Grain Initiative and its November renewal—real favorable evidence. The Istanbul drafts show mutual movement on neutrality and security guarantees, but unresolved territory, force limits, guarantee mechanics, and sequencing; there was no completed peace agreement that one outside actor simply vetoed. The reported Kozak proposal is relevant but remains anonymously sourced and unverified as to Ukrainian final acceptance. Annexation and mobilization later narrowed the bargaining space (WEB-2B-013–015).

### 2B.8 — Spending, mobilization, patronage, and intimidation

Escalatory use is established; enrichment motives are not. SIPRI estimated Russian military expenditure rose 9.2% in real terms to about $86.4 billion and 4.1% of GDP; actual national-defence spending substantially exceeded prewar plans. Putin ordered partial mobilization after major battlefield setbacks. Available sources do not establish what portion enriched personal networks or whether procurement corruption motivated the war, so that part remains unresolved (WEB-2B-015).

### 2B.9 — Accountability and correction

Strong evidence of non-correction at the ruler level. Russia continued operations despite the ICJ order, denied or deflected many abuse findings, restricted independent access, and produced no credible public investigation assigning senior responsibility during 2022. Putin escalated through mobilization and annexation rather than acknowledging initial military assumptions or civilian consequences. The later ICC warrant is not a conviction, but provides direct reasonable-grounds attribution concerning child transfer (WEB-2B-006, 009–012, 015, 017).

### 2B.10 — Peaceful, stable, lawful end state

Strong adverse outcome evidence. The year ended with an interstate war estimated by UCDP at more than 81,500 battle deaths, attempted annexations rejected by 143 UN members, 11.6 million Ukrainians still displaced, higher Russian and European military spending, and Finnish and Swedish NATO applications. Causation is shared after initiation—Ukraine resisted and Western states armed it—but the change from the January baseline is inseparable from Putin’s invasion decision (WEB-2B-005, 007–008, 015–016).

## 4. Source-state audit

### Independent corroboration

- UN General Assembly resolutions ES-11/1 and ES-11/4 corroborate the legal and territorial characterization independently of Ukrainian claims.
- OHCHR’s monitoring mission, its December killings investigation, and the independent Commission overlap institutionally but used different mandates and evidence collections.
- ICRC access evidence is independent of OHCHR abuse interviews.
- UCDP’s fatality dataset, UNHCR displacement totals, SIPRI expenditure data, and NATO accession records measure different consequences.
- Putin’s official speeches corroborate personal decision and justification, but not the truth of those justifications.
- Reuters’ Kozak account relied on anonymous leadership-adjacent sources and is not independent of later articles repeating it.

### Reused supplied sources

- **E001:** Kremlin authority page. Reused in concept; direct opening timed out, replaced by the Russian Supreme Court’s constitutional text.
- **E005:** ICJ order. Reused; direct page was blocked, but the searchable official order text and operative paragraphs were extracted.
- **E011:** UN Commission graphic report. Reused; PDF direct opening was blocked, but official indexed text was extracted and checked against OHCHR reporting.
- **E012:** ICC Ukraine situation page. Reused; direct opening was blocked, official indexed text extracted.
- **E039:** ICRC POW-access statement. Fully opened and extracted.
- **E040:** Grain Initiative. Reused and strengthened with UN signing, operational, and renewal pages.
- **E041:** UN Security Council diplomatic-channel coverage. Retained as corroboration; OSCE and NATO records supply the stronger baseline facts.
- **E047:** Treasury account of separatist recognition. Not used as final evidence because primary Kremlin and UN records were preferable.
- **E050:** Responsible Statecraft/Reuters summary. Retained only as a limited negotiation lead; the Reuters wire reproduction was inspected.

### Promising but still needing inspection

- Full Russian and Ukrainian spring 2022 draft-treaty texts and Istanbul communiqué variants.
- Russian General Staff operational directives and rules of engagement.
- Russian military-prosecutor files concerning Bucha, POW treatment, filtration, and child transfers.
- Detailed 2022 procurement and beneficial-ownership records.
- UCDP event-level download for actor-by-actor 2022 fatality coding.
- PA-X coding for any 2022 Russia–Ukraine humanitarian or prisoner arrangements.

### Rejected sources

- Wikipedia summaries: useful for discovery, not accepted as evidence.
- Reddit reproductions of Reuters and Kremlin material: derivative and unnecessary.
- Belligerent casualty totals from either defence ministry: not independently verifiable.
- U.S. Treasury characterizations of Putin’s rationale as “false”: replaced where possible by ICJ and UN material.
- Claims that Boris Johnson alone “blocked” a completed Istanbul agreement: the evidence reviewed shows unresolved substantive and guarantee issues.
- Claims that Minsk signatories’ later statements prove the agreements were solely a deception: retrospective political statements do not independently establish all parties’ 2014–2022 intent.

### Access-blocked

- ICJ node page: repeated direct opening returned HTTP 403; official search extraction exposed the order’s operative text.
- OHCHR Commission graphic PDF: direct opening returned HTTP 403; official indexed extraction and parallel OHCHR pages were available.
- ICC Ukraine situation page: direct opening returned HTTP 403; official indexed extraction was available.
- Several Kremlin transcript pages: direct requests timed out; Kremlin index/search extracts and independent transcript reproductions were used cautiously.
- SIPRI publication landing page intermittently timed out; the fact sheet, press release, and PDF-indexed text remained available.

## 5. Source counts

Counting distinct URLs, not repeated search appearances:

- Discovered: **69**
- Opened or fully rendered for extraction: **31**
- Accepted as underlying evidence: **21 sources supporting 17 records**
- Supplied sources reused: **9**, of which 5 contributed directly and 4 were replaced or retained as corroboration
- Rejected: **6 source families**
- Access-blocked: **5 URL families**

“Accepted” exceeds the number of records because several records use separate underlying and corroborating sources.

## 6. Remaining questions and saturation

Further public-source research is unlikely to change the central findings on initiation, annexation, civilian harm, mobilization, or year-end instability. It could materially improve:

- the precise internal decision process before 24 February;
- Putin’s knowledge of particular operational crimes;
- actual proxy command-and-control arrangements;
- whether a specific spring settlement was acceptable to both governments at the same time;
- procurement enrichment and military-patronage beneficiaries;
- remedial orders, if any, issued privately.

Those gaps chiefly affect attribution granularity and lenses 2B.5, 2B.8, and 2B.9. They do not erase the well-documented strategic decisions.

## Artifact Y

 ## Orientation

Vladimir Putin held decisive formal authority in 2022: Russia’s Constitution made the president Supreme Commander-in-Chief, while practical evidence directly attributes recognition of the separatist entities, the invasion announcement, mobilization, annexation, and nuclear signaling to him.

The inherited baseline was not peaceful. Russia had annexed Crimea in 2014 and supported armed formations in Donbas; ceasefire violations rose sharply immediately before the invasion. That constituted a real security and conflict-management problem, but not proof of a Ukrainian genocidal attack or of defensive necessity for a nationwide invasion. NATO offered continued negotiations on arms control, transparency, risk reduction, and European security in January 2022. The ICJ subsequently ordered Russia to suspend the operations begun on 24 February, while the UN General Assembly characterized them as aggression and later rejected Russia’s attempted annexations.

The adverse evidence is unusually strong: initiation of a cross-border war, rejected or abandoned peaceful alternatives, territorial annexation, unsupported public claims of genocide, foreseeable civilian harm, documented killings and mistreatment, inadequate POW access, rising military expenditure and mobilization, noncompliance with the ICJ order, and no demonstrated corrective accountability. UCDP estimated more than 81,500 battle deaths in the interstate conflict during 2022; UNHCR counted 11.6 million Ukrainians still displaced at year-end.

Material favorable evidence exists but is narrower: Russia articulated genuine NATO-related security concerns; participated in negotiations where neutrality was discussed; joined and renewed the Black Sea Grain Initiative; and authorized significant prisoner exchanges. These measures mitigated discrete harms but did not stop or reverse the wider war. The alleged rejection by Putin of an early Kozak-negotiated neutrality arrangement is important but rests on anonymous-source reporting and remains disputed.

Russia’s wartime censorship and restrictions on dissent make domestic silence weak evidence. Operational archives, deliberative records, procurement audits, and complete proxy-control records remain unavailable.

## Ten-question disposition

1. **2B.1 — Diplomacy and restraint:** Predominantly adverse. WEB-2B-005 establishes credible diplomatic alternatives; WEB-2B-013 and WEB-2B-E040 show that negotiated action was possible. WEB-2B-E050 is potentially important but disputed.

2. **2B.2 — War, coercion and annexation:** Strong adverse evidence. WEB-2B-006, WEB-2B-E005, WEB-2B-008 and WEB-2B-009 establish ruler-directed initiation, continuation despite legal demand, and attempted annexation.

3. **2B.3 — Genuine security versus manufactured or expansionary objectives:** Mixed inputs, adverse balance. NATO expansion and Donbas violence were genuine concerns (WEB-2B-002, WEB-2B-005), but nationwide invasion, “denazification,” unsupported genocide claims and annexation exceeded the demonstrated defensive problem (WEB-2B-006, WEB-2B-009).

4. **2B.4 — Civilian protection and prisoner treatment:** Strong adverse evidence, with limited counterevidence of Ukrainian violations. See WEB-2B-E011, WEB-2B-011, WEB-2B-E039 and WEB-2B-E012.

5. **2B.5 — Control of forces, affiliates and proxies:** Adverse institutional evidence but incomplete ruler-specific command evidence. Repeated and patterned conduct, foreseeable energy-system harm, incomplete POW access, lack of investigation and the ICC warrant support responsibility; internal orders and full DPR/LPR/Wagner command records remain unavailable.

6. **2B.6 — Truthful public explanation:** Adverse. Putin personally invoked genocide, “denazification,” NATO danger and Article 51; neither the monitored inherited casualty pattern nor international proceedings substantiated genocide. Later nuclear language served coercion in the annexation context.

7. **2B.7 — Ceasefires and settlements:** Mixed but adverse overall. Russia entered talks and humanitarian arrangements, including the grain corridor, but did not conclude a general ceasefire, complied with neither the ICJ suspension order nor the UN withdrawal demand, and continued annexation and mobilization.

8. **2B.8 — Spending, mobilization and patronage:** Spending and mobilization evidence is adverse as to war expansion: WEB-2B-004 and WEB-2B-016. Evidence is insufficient to determine what share enriched patronage networks or personally rewarded security elites; budget opacity lowers confidence.

9. **2B.9 — Accountability:** Strong adverse evidence. Russia continued operations after the ICJ order, denied independently documented conduct, and OHCHR found no meaningful Russian investigation of killings. The ICC warrant is a reasonable-grounds determination, not a conviction.

10. **2B.10 — End-state peace, stability and legality:** Strong adverse outcome. WEB-2B-003 and WEB-2B-019 show exceptionally high fatalities and displacement; WEB-2B-008 and WEB-2B-009 establish deterioration in Charter compliance and territorial stability.

## Source disposition

**Independent corroboration**

- Initiation and illegality: ICJ order; UNGA ES-11/1; UNGA ES-11/4.
- Civilian conduct: OHCHR monitoring; UN Commission of Inquiry; ICRC detention-access reporting; later ICC proceedings.
- Scale and end-state: UCDP fatality estimates; UNHCR displacement statistics; SIPRI spending estimates.
- Negotiation: NATO’s contemporaneous proposals; Ukraine’s public Istanbul proposal; UN/PA-X grain records.

**Reused and reopened**

- E001, E005, E011, E012, E039, E040 and E050.
- E050 was retained as disputed reporting, not elevated to a final legal or documentary finding.

**Uninspected leads**

- Full Russian Treasury execution documents underlying SIPRI’s estimate.
- Classified Russian General Staff operational orders and rules of engagement.
- Complete Istanbul draft texts and negotiation notes.
- DPR/LPR, Chechen formation and Wagner command-and-discipline records.
- Procurement, beneficiary and military-industrial patronage audits.
- Full Russian military justice docket for Ukraine-related allegations.

**Rejected**

- Wikipedia and Reddit reproductions: discovery only.
- Belligerent casualty totals lacking independent methodology.
- Claims that NATO or Boris Johnson definitively “blocked” a completed peace treaty: evidence does not establish a finalized enforceable agreement or singular external veto.
- Claims that all civilian casualties were intentionally targeted: broader than the findings inspected.
- Treating the ICC warrant as a conviction: procedurally incorrect.
- Treating aggregate military expenditure as proof of personal enrichment: unsupported inference.

**Blocked or partial**

- Kremlin presidential-structure page: timed out; authority resolved through the Russian Supreme Court’s constitutional text.
- ICJ and ICC direct opening initially returned access errors; indexed official pages were subsequently inspected.
- Supplied UN Black Sea FAQ initially returned 403; official UN indexed page and related contemporaneous statements were inspected.
- Supplied OHCHR graphic PDF initially returned 403; official indexed report text and full-report search extraction were inspected.
- Supplied UN Security Council page returned 404.

## Counts and saturation

- Distinct pages or documents discovered: 59.
- Accepted atomic evidence records: 19.
- Reused supplied records: 7.
- Independent corroborating source families: 9.
- Uninspected material leads: 6 categories.
- Rejected source/claim categories: 6.
- Blocked or initially partial sources: 5.

Further research is most likely to add material information on Istanbul negotiations, operational command responsibility, proxy discipline, military procurement/patronage, and internal accountability. It is less likely to change the well-corroborated conclusions concerning initiation, annexation, the scale of harm, or the year-end deterioration in peace and legality.

# Olaf Scholz — 2B (Germany)


## Artifact X

 ## Scope and orientation

Period assessed: Olaf Scholz, Germany, 1 January–31 December 2022. No score is assigned.

### Authority

Scholz had substantial but not unitary security authority. Under Basic Law article 65, the chancellor sets the government’s general policy guidelines and is responsible for them; ministers independently administer their portfolios. In peacetime, command over the Bundeswehr belonged to the defence minister, not Scholz. Bundestag authorization was required for armed deployments abroad. Arms-export decisions were institutionally shared among ministries and, for sensitive cases, the Federal Security Council, which Scholz chaired. [Basic Law, arts. 65, 65a and 87a](https://www.bundestag.de/parlament/aufgaben/rechtsgrundlagen/grundgesetz/?id=5591).

Accordingly:

- Scholz is directly attributable for his public threat characterization, the *Zeitenwende* policy line, diplomacy with Putin, and advocacy of the €100 billion fund.
- Ukraine arms support, sanctions and export licensing carry shared executive, coalition, EU/NATO or parliamentary attribution.
- Individual Ukrainian or Malian operations cannot be attributed to Scholz without evidence of German operational control, direction or knowing toleration.

### Baseline, exposure and shocks

Germany entered 2022 outside any UCDP state-based armed conflict as a belligerent, but with inherited overseas deployments, principally UN stabilization in Mali and NATO reassurance missions. The UCDP 23.1 dataset is the contemporaneous release covering conflicts through 2022; the public API queries intended to verify a Germany-specific zero returned access errors, so the D13 country extraction remains a technical gap rather than an asserted numerical finding. [UCDP replication archive](https://ucdp.uu.se/downloads/replication_data.html).

The central shock was Russia’s 24 February full-scale invasion of Ukraine, expanding the conflict begun in 2014. Germany became a major supplier and sanctioning state but did not send combat forces into Ukraine. Other constraints included coalition government, Bundestag control of deployments and budgets, NATO coordination, depleted Bundeswehr stocks, longstanding Russian-energy dependence, escalation fears, Mali’s military government and Wagner partnership, and secrecy around weapons deliveries.

The SIPRI D14 baseline was German military spending of $56.0 billion in 2021, 1.3% of GDP. In 2022 it was $55.8 billion, 1.4% of GDP and 2.3% higher in real terms. Thus the announced €100 billion fund represented a major future commitment, but not an equivalent cash-spending jump during 2022. [SIPRI 2021 fact sheet](https://www.sipri.org/sites/default/files/2022-04/fs_2204_milex_2021.pdf), [SIPRI 2022 fact sheet](https://www.sipri.org/sites/default/files/2023-04/2304_fs_milex_2022.pdf).

### Information environment

Germany’s open parliament, opposition, media and courts produced unusually visible criticism, including allegations that Scholz overstated or obscured weapons-delivery progress. Delivery secrecy had a credible operational-security rationale, but it also prevented complete contemporaneous verification. Government speeches reliably establish what Scholz declared, not whether every threat inference or implementation claim was correct.

## Ten-lens disposition

### 2B.1 — Diplomacy versus force

Predominantly favorable, with important limits. Before the invasion, Scholz participated in NATO–Russia diplomacy, warned Putin personally in Moscow and initially maintained Germany’s restrictive arms policy. After invasion, he used sanctions and arms support without deploying German combat forces to Ukraine and kept direct channels open (WEB-2B-004–008, WEB-2B-019).

The contrary case is that prewar German policy may have underestimated Russian coercion, while post-invasion delays in supplying defensive weapons may have reduced Ukraine’s ability to resist rather than serving productive de-escalation (WEB-2B-014). Evidence does not establish that Scholz rejected a credible peace agreement in favor of war.

### 2B.2 — Wars of choice, coercion or proxy conflict

No evidence that Scholz initiated a war of choice, annexation, covert destabilization or cross-border combat operation in 2022. Germany’s support followed Russia’s invasion and was publicly framed around Ukraine’s UN Charter self-defence. Germany nevertheless became a substantial arms supplier, creating indirect conflict exposure (WEB-2B-007, WEB-2B-011).

The inherited Mali deployment was renewed and its troop ceiling increased, but it remained Bundestag-approved participation in a UN mission, not a German war of conquest (WEB-2B-017).

### 2B.3 — Defensive need versus prestige or manufactured threat

The defensive rationale had strong independent grounding: the Russian troop buildup was observable before 24 February, and Russia then invaded and attempted territorial seizure. NATO reassurance deployments remained on allied territory. SIPRI also found that European spending increases were widely driven by the invasion (WEB-2B-004, WEB-2B-006, WEB-2B-009–010).

Scholz’s assertion that Putin sought to reconstruct an empire was an interpretive judgment rather than a judicial fact. The spending package may also have reflected inherited procurement failures and alliance burden-sharing. No evidence located ties it to personal prestige, regime survival or manufactured intelligence.

### 2B.4 — Civilian protection and humanitarian law

Germany did not conduct combat operations in Ukraine, so there is no German target-selection or prisoner-treatment record to assess there. It supplied air defence, vehicles, artillery and other weapons while also funding humanitarian and medical relief and pursuing accountability for international crimes (WEB-2B-011, WEB-2B-015–016).

In Mali, German forces served within MINUSMA while Malian and Wagner forces committed extensive abuses. The available evidence does not connect German troops to those atrocities, but it makes recipient-force diligence an important concern (WEB-2B-017–018). Silence about German civilian harm is meaningful only for the limited operational roles documented, not proof that every deployment was harm-free.

### 2B.5 — Restraint of allies, proxies and arms recipients

Mixed. Germany had formal licensing, re-export, human-rights review and post-shipment-control mechanisms (WEB-2B-012). It reduced EUTM Mali activity as abuses and Wagner cooperation escalated, while retaining MINUSMA peacekeeping (WEB-2B-018).

However, the 2022 export report disclosed approvals to Saudi Arabia, including fire-control equipment and cannon and aircraft-related components, notwithstanding the kingdom’s recent Yemen-war role (WEB-2B-013). No source inspected demonstrated Ukraine-specific on-site post-shipment inspections in 2022, or a Scholz-directed system to investigate every allegation involving German-supplied weapons. Recipient-control effectiveness therefore remains only partly established.

### 2B.6 — Truthful public explanation

Scholz accurately identified Russia as the invader, Ukraine as the state defending its territory, and the attack as a breach of sovereignty and the UN order; the invasion itself independently corroborates the central claims (WEB-2B-006). He did not manufacture an attack on Germany or claim German combat was necessary.

His public communications about the pace and scope of weapons assistance were credibly contested. Reporting alleged that the Chancellery removed systems from a Ukrainian request list and that official descriptions created a more expansive impression than implementation supported (WEB-2B-014). That establishes a transparency and possible spin concern, not a proven intelligence fabrication comparable to a manufactured casus belli.

### 2B.7 — Ceasefires, talks and lawful settlements

Scholz met Putin before the invasion, kept communication channels open afterward, and continued to call for Russian withdrawal and a settlement preserving Ukrainian sovereignty (WEB-2B-005, WEB-2B-008, WEB-2B-019). His refusal to treat Ukrainian capitulation as viable is consistent with a lawful-settlement position.

No German-brokered ceasefire or settlement resulted in 2022. The evidence does not show that Scholz controlled the Russia–Ukraine negotiations or that a specific mutually acceptable agreement was available and rejected by him (WEB-2B-020).

### 2B.8 — Spending, mobilization and patronage

The €100 billion special fund and planned spending above 2% were linked publicly to Russia’s invasion, Bundeswehr readiness failures and NATO territorial defence. Actual SIPRI spending rose only 2.3% in real terms in 2022, undermining any claim that the entire announced fund was immediately spent (WEB-2B-002–003, WEB-2B-009).

No evidence located shows the fund was designed to enrich Scholz associates, reward a personal security elite or intimidate neighboring states. Procurement efficiency and industrial benefits deserve separate auditing, but they are not evidence of personal patronage by themselves.

### 2B.9 — Accountability

Germany referred the Ukraine situation to the ICC, and the Federal Public Prosecutor opened a structural investigation into war crimes and later crimes against humanity. These were institutional rather than solely Scholz-personal actions, but they occurred under his government (WEB-2B-016).

There was no comparable adjudicated German operational failure in Ukraine for Scholz to acknowledge. His government did face criticism over delivery delays and inherited Bundeswehr deficiencies, but the 2022 record contains little evidence of a formal Scholz apology, independent inquiry or remedy focused on those communication failures. Accountability evidence is therefore favorable on international crimes but incomplete on his own implementation record.

### 2B.10 — End-state peace, stability and legality

The end state was materially less peaceful than the inherited January 2022 baseline: war continued, deaths and displacement increased, Russia–Germany relations collapsed and European militarization accelerated. Those developments were driven principally by Russia’s invasion, not initiated by Scholz. Germany helped sustain Ukraine’s survival, NATO cohesion, humanitarian relief and international-criminal accountability, but did not achieve a ceasefire or settlement (WEB-2B-006–011, WEB-2B-015–016, WEB-2B-020).

The appropriate conclusion is therefore constrained and non-causal: Scholz’s policies largely defended the existing territorial and legal order, but the regional system he left at year-end was objectively more violent, militarized and unstable.

## Source-state accounting

### Opened corroboration

- NATO transcript corroborating an active January diplomatic track and the visible Russian buildup.
- SIPRI 2021 and 2022 expenditure fact sheets.
- Bundestag Basic Law and Mali mandate records.
- 2022 German Arms Export Report.
- HRW’s 2022 Mali findings.
- Bundestag and Foreign Office records concerning German war-crimes investigations.
- Guardian, AP/Reuters-derived reporting, OSW and Carnegie analyses on weapons delays and strategic caution.
- UCDP replication archive establishing the relevant dataset version and temporal coverage.

### Reused supplied sources

Opened and reused: E001, E002, E003, E004, E006, E029, E031, E032, E035, E036 and E037. E007 repeats E004’s aid and sanctions chronology and was retained as corroboration rather than a new underlying fact. E008 lacked a sufficiently precise locator and was not used as a separate record. E030 and E056 were substantively corroborated by the export report and official delivery records.

### Uninspected or partly inspected leads

- Raw UCDP 23.1 CSV/API country extraction for a formal Germany-specific D13 zero.
- Bundestag Drucksache 20/1761 annexes concerning detailed MINUSMA rules and force protection.
- Classified Ukraine delivery and end-use records.
- Federal Security Council minutes for individual Saudi approvals.
- Recipient-level evidence linking particular German-trained Malian units to particular abuses.
- Procurement-contract audits for the Bundeswehr special fund.
- Full PA-X search for agreements in which Germany had a 2022 mediation role; no prominent Germany-brokered Ukraine agreement surfaced.

### Rejected

- Wikipedia pages: useful for leads but unnecessary where primary or independent sources were available.
- Reddit timelines alleging “lies”: advocacy compilations dependent on press stories and often conflating delay with falsification.
- Post-2022 Leopard and Taurus decisions: outside the target period.
- Later Saudi missile approvals from 2023–24: outside the period.
- Generic reports of Ukrainian or Russian violations without a connection to German policy or supplied systems.
- Country-wide German human-rights indicators unrelated to international peace and security.

### Access-blocked

- UCDP API Germany event and battle-death queries: server/internal errors.
- SIPRI PDF opening occasionally returned an internal error; indexed PDF text and the publication landing page remained available.
- OSW article opening returned a cache miss; search extraction was retained only as corroboration.
- BMWK press-release opening failed; the underlying Bundestag export-report PDF opened successfully.
- ICC situation page opening failed in one attempt; indexed ICC material and German official records corroborated the referral.

### Counts

Counting unique underlying URLs encountered rather than search-result duplicates:

- Discovered: 54
- Opened or text-extracted: 29
- Accepted as fully developed evidence: 20
- Reused from the supplied list: 11 of the 20 accepted records
- Rejected: 6
- Access-blocked or materially incomplete: 5
- Remaining uninspected leads: 6

“Reused” is a subset of accepted, not an additional source total.

## Remaining questions and saturation

Further open-source work is most likely to add material information in four narrow areas:

- the formal Germany-specific UCDP country extraction;
- Ukraine-specific end-use monitoring and responses to recipient misconduct;
- Federal Security Council reasoning for Saudi approvals;
- procurement integrity and delivery performance under the special fund.

More general searching is unlikely to change the central account: extensive pre-invasion diplomacy; a defensive, alliance-coordinated response after an externally initiated invasion; substantial but sometimes delayed and opaquely communicated arms support; formal recipient-control and accountability systems with important gaps; inherited UN operations in Mali amid recipient-force atrocities; and no peaceful end state by December 2022.

## Artifact Y

 ## Orientation

**Period:** 1 January–31 December 2022. Olaf Scholz became chancellor on 8 December 2021, so this is effectively his first full calendar year.

**Authority:** Scholz determined the government’s general policy guidelines, chaired the cabinet and participated in the Federal Security Council, but he was not the Bundeswehr’s peacetime operational commander; that authority belonged to Defence Minister Christine Lambrecht. Armed foreign deployments normally also required Bundestag approval. Arms-export decisions were shared among ministries and, for sensitive cases, the Federal Security Council. The Bundestag approved and scrutinised foreign missions and the €100 billion special fund.

**Conflict/exposure gate:** Germany did not deploy combat forces into Ukraine or become a NATO belligerent there. Its exposure was nevertheless substantial: weapons and training for Ukraine, sanctions and economic coercion against Russia, deterrence deployments on NATO territory, inherited UN/NATO/EU missions, arms exports to other states, and significant diplomatic influence. Germany therefore is not a “no-exposure” case.

**D13 UCDP qualification:** UCDP’s current country-year API requires an access token, and the downloadable Germany row could not be opened through the available interface. Consequently, this dossier does **not** promote an inferred zero-conflict value into an extracted UCDP statistic. The operational record independently shows no German combat deployment in Ukraine, while the Parliamentary Commissioner recorded nine other Bundestag-mandated missions at year-end. The unresolved UCDP row is listed as an uninspected lead.

**D14 SIPRI facts:** SIPRI estimated German military expenditure at **$55.8 billion in 2022**, 2.3% higher in real terms than 2021 and 33% higher than 2013; this included about $2 billion in military aid to Ukraine. The €100 billion fund was largely intended for use from 2023. Germany was also the fifth-largest exporter of major arms over 2018–22. [SIPRI expenditure](https://www.sipri.org/sites/default/files/2023-04/2304_fs_milex_2022.pdf), [SIPRI transfers](https://www.sipri.org/publications/2023/sipri-fact-sheets/trends-international-arms-transfers-2022).

**Inherited baseline and shocks:** Scholz inherited Russia’s 2014 annexation of Crimea, the Donbas conflict and faltering Minsk/Normandy process; long-standing NATO, Kosovo, Mali, Iraq, Lebanon and Mediterranean commitments; depleted Bundeswehr stocks; and heavy Russian-energy dependence. Russia’s 24 February invasion, mass displacement, attacks on civilians and energy infrastructure, nuclear-escalation risk and the collapse of German assumptions about economic interdependence dominated the year.

**Information conditions:** Germany had open parliamentary and media contestation: Scholz was criticised both for excessive caution and for escalation through arms transfers. Operational stocks, intelligence, arms-delivery details and Federal Security Council deliberations were less observable. Official speeches reliably establish decisions and stated purposes, but not efficacy or private motives.

**Central balance, without a score:** The strongest favorable evidence is pre-invasion diplomacy; the absence of German combat entry; insistence on Ukrainian self-determination and territorial integrity; rejection of annexation; escalation limits; defensive and humanitarian support; continued contacts with Putin; support for the ICJ, ICC and OSCE investigations; and participation in multilateral peacekeeping. Adverse or limiting evidence includes the scale of rearmament, opaque and initially flawed special-fund planning, arms exports to Saudi Arabia and other high-risk recipients, weak public evidence of recipient-specific end-use/IHL conditions, and delay or opacity in some Ukraine deliveries. The regional end-state was dramatically less peaceful, principally because of Russia’s invasion; Germany helped contain wider NATO–Russia war but did not secure a 2022 ceasefire or settlement.

## Ten-question disposition

1. **2B.1 — Substantial favorable evidence, with a contested post-invasion balance.** Scholz pursued NATO–Russia, OSCE, Normandy and bilateral diplomacy before the invasion (WEB-2B-004), kept channels open afterward (WEB-2B-008), and imposed escalation limits (WEB-2B-007). Critics argued that arms deliveries displaced diplomacy or risked escalation; others argued his delays weakened Ukraine’s defensive position (WEB-2B-018).

2. **2B.2 — No evidence that Scholz initiated a war of choice or German cross-border combat campaign.** Germany supplied Ukraine’s defence and strengthened NATO territory but explicitly avoided NATO combat entry (WEB-2B-005–007). Other deployments were inherited and multilaterally mandated (WEB-2B-016). Arms support materially affected a war but followed Russia’s invasion.

3. **2B.3 — Generally clear separation of defensive aims from conquest or revenge.** Scholz framed the threat as forced border change and imperial restoration, limited Germany’s objective to Ukraine’s defence and NATO security, rejected a dictated peace and refused direct NATO war (WEB-2B-005, 007, 009). His historical rhetoric was forceful but tied to observable Russian conduct, not a manufactured German grievance.

4. **2B.4 — No German combat conduct in Ukraine to assess directly.** Germany funded humanitarian and medical assistance and supplied air defence (WEB-2B-010). Recipient forces nevertheless committed documented violations involving civilian positioning and POW treatment (WEB-2B-011–012). No inspected source tied a specific German weapon to those abuses.

5. **2B.5 — Mixed and incompletely observable.** Export licensing and multilateral coordination supplied some restraint, and the early Saudi ban showed recipient-risk awareness (WEB-2B-017). But public evidence did not demonstrate that Scholz conditioned Ukrainian aid on specific remedial IHL measures after documented violations. Germany remained a major global exporter (WEB-2B-003) and approved cooperation-related Saudi exports.

6. **2B.6 — Public justification was broadly corroborated, though official rhetoric cannot prove private motive.** Russian military buildup and the invasion itself corroborated Scholz’s warnings; his description of an unlawful attack matched UN/OHCHR findings. His claim that Putin sought imperial revision was interpretive but grounded in Russia’s annexation attempts and invasion (WEB-2B-004–005). No evidence emerged of fabricated German intelligence or a knowingly false casus belli.

7. **2B.7 — Credible diplomatic and legal efforts, but no settlement achieved.** Scholz sought de-escalation before invasion, repeatedly called for ceasefire/withdrawal, kept contact with Putin, supported a UN-brokered grain agreement and insisted Ukraine control its peace terms (WEB-2B-004, 008–009). Germany supported the ICJ and post-war reconstruction (WEB-2B-014). Evidence does not show that Scholz possessed and rejected a clearly viable 2022 settlement.

8. **2B.8 — Real security rationale with important governance weaknesses.** Russia’s invasion, depleted stocks and alliance obligations supply a concrete security basis for the special fund and spending increase (WEB-2B-002, 007). The Federal Audit Office found overplanning, omitted debt-service costs and opaque aggregate titles (WEB-2B-015). No inspected evidence establishes personal enrichment or rewarding a Scholz patronage network.

9. **2B.9 — Institutional accountability was meaningful but incomplete.** Germany supported OSCE, ICC and ICJ mechanisms (WEB-2B-013–014), parliamentary scrutiny was open, and Scholz publicly acknowledged inadequate ammunition preparation (WEB-2B-007). There was no German operational civilian-harm finding requiring personal admission. Public evidence of a Scholz response to Ukrainian recipient violations was thin.

10. **2B.10 — Outcome is externally dominated and cannot be characterised as a peaceful improvement.** Europe ended 2022 at large-scale war, with strained German–Russian relations and greater militarisation. Russia’s invasion was the primary cause. Germany helped prevent direct NATO–Russia combat, sustained multilateral missions, supported refugees, humanitarian relief and reconstruction, and strengthened NATO deterrence (WEB-2B-007, 010, 016), but did not end the war.

## Source disposition

**Independent corroboration**

- SIPRI independently corroborates German spending and military-aid magnitude.
- NATO records corroborate the absence of NATO combat forces and Germany’s eastern-flank deployments.
- Reuters and the Guardian corroborate open criticism, delivery delays and stated capacity constraints.
- Amnesty and OHCHR independently document recipient-side IHL risks.
- The Federal Audit Office independently identified special-fund defects.
- Bundestag records corroborate parliamentary authority, delivery categories and dissent.

**Reused and reopened**

- E001 → WEB-2B-005.
- E002 → WEB-2B-007 and WEB-2B-015’s policy baseline.
- E003/E036 → WEB-2B-006.
- E004/E007 → WEB-2B-010.
- E031 → WEB-2B-018.
- E035 → information-environment corroboration.
- E037 → supporting export data incorporated with WEB-2B-006.
- E055 → contrary lead on negotiation viability; target-period statements were preferred.
- E056 → reopened as implementation corroboration, not a separate atomic record.

**Uninspected leads**

- UCDP Organized Violence Country-Year Germany 2022 row: API token required; no extracted D13 value.
- PA-X: no Germany-led 2022 Ukraine peace agreement identified.
- Classified Federal Security Council minutes and intelligence assessments.
- Recipient-specific German end-use monitoring reports for Ukraine.
- Full weapon-level linkage between German-origin equipment and particular 2022 incidents.

**Rejected**

- Wikipedia, Reddit and derivative aggregation pages: discovery only.
- Later Gaza/Israel export controversies: outside the 2022 period.
- 2023–26 Saudi and Ukraine decisions: excluded except where they directly reported final 2022 statistics.
- Belligerent Russian claims about “provocations” and Ukrainian “capitulation”: not used without independent corroboration.
- Opinion polling: useful for political context but not evidence of lawful security conduct.

**Blocked or partial**

- Official Basic Law PDF/individual-article pages timed out; their content was cross-checked against Bundestag and Bundeswehr summaries but was not counted as a fully extracted evidence record.
- Supplied English donors-conference archive URL failed; the underlying German-government news page was opened instead.
- SIPRI’s HTML expenditure page timed out; its official PDF text was inspectable.
- The Federal Audit Office English page intermittently failed; the same official German page was inspected through search extraction and is paired with the record’s limitation.
- Several official phone-call archive URLs were not indexed; contemporaneous Tagesschau reporting of the government and Kremlin summaries was used.

**Discovery/disposition counts**

- Approximately 72 distinct candidate pages surfaced.
- 41 underlying pages or PDFs were opened successfully.
- 18 facts accepted as machine records.
- 9 additional pages used only as corroboration.
- 8 uninspected or access-limited leads.
- 12 rejected as derivative, out-of-period, duplicative or insufficiently corroborated.
- 5 material access failures/partial inspections.

## Remaining questions and saturation

Further research could materially improve three areas:

- an authenticated extraction of Germany’s 2022 UCDP country-year row;
- classified or parliamentary-confidential evidence on Ukraine end-use monitoring and Federal Security Council reasoning;
- recipient-specific evidence connecting German arms, training or diplomatic leverage to corrective action after Ukrainian IHL violations.

Open-source searching otherwise reached practical saturation: further results largely repeated the diplomacy, arms chronology, spending, escalation-restraint and accountability evidence already captured.

# Abdel Fattah el-Sisi — 3B (Egypt)


## Artifact X

 ## Scope and orientation

This assessment covers calendar year 2022 only. Abdel Fattah el-Sisi was president throughout the period.

### Authority and practical control

Egypt’s constitution made the president head of state, supreme commander of the armed forces, chair of the National Defence Council, appointing authority for senior civil and military officials, and a necessary reviewing authority for military-court and emergency-court judgments. The 2019 amendments also strengthened presidential influence over senior judicial appointments. This supports strong attribution for presidential pardons, national security policy, emergency-law substitutes, senior appointments, and military policy. It does not establish that Sisi personally ordered every arrest, custodial abuse, or local police action.

Practical coercive power was distributed among the Interior Ministry, National Security Agency, General Intelligence Service, armed forces, military intelligence, prison administration, Supreme State Security Prosecution, and an executive-influenced judiciary. These were durable institutions with operational autonomy, but the evidence shows neither meaningful civilian independence from the presidency nor an effective institution capable of holding politically protected security personnel accountable.

### Inherited baseline

Sisi entered 2022 after nearly nine years of rule, not as a new ruler inheriting another government’s system. The relevant baseline was therefore largely his own accumulated record:

- mass political imprisonment and prolonged pretrial detention;
- recurring enforced disappearance and torture allegations;
- emergency courts and terrorism prosecutions used against peaceful critics;
- prisons largely closed to independent monitoring;
- a long-running North Sinai insurgency accompanied by displacement, demolition, civilian-harm allegations, and severe reporting restrictions;
- a public-order system in which peaceful protest had become rare because of arrest risk.

The nationwide state of emergency formally ended in October 2021, but contemporaneous legal reporting found that legislation enacted around its termination retained analogous presidential and military powers.

### Shocks and constraints

The continuing ISIS-Sinai insurgency was a real physical-security threat. The State Department counted at least 95 reported terrorist incidents and roughly 260 casualties in Sinai, while also finding a decline in terrorist activity compared with previous years. Its report expressly warned that casualty figures could not be confirmed because of restricted reporting. The Russia–Ukraine and currency shocks contributed to November protest calls, but do not excuse preventive arrests of peaceful critics and are not otherwise treated as chapter evidence.

### Information environment

Independent access to prisons and North Sinai was highly restricted. Domestic monitors operated under surveillance, prosecution, travel bans, asset freezes, and closure pressure. Complaint and arrest counts are documented cases, not prevalence estimates. Sparse reporting cannot be treated as evidence of safety.

The supplied local D12 prior says PTS, CIRIGHTS, V-Dem and UCDP all establish a severe national physical-integrity/repression context, but no underlying country-year rows were supplied, and I could not reliably extract exact 2022 values from their live interfaces. I therefore retain that prior as context, not as a fully extracted evidence record. UCDP’s thresholded one-sided-violence series also should not be treated as a complete measure of custodial deaths, disappearance, torture, or individual executions.

## Compact evidence index

- **WEB-3B-001 — Presidential coercive authority:** Establishes formal responsibility for national security policy, senior appointments, pardons, and exceptional-court review.
- **WEB-3B-002 — Broad physical-integrity abuse and impunity:** Cross-sector annual finding covering killings, disappearance, torture, detention, and ineffective accountability.
- **WEB-3B-003 — Hadhoud death and failed investigation:** Detailed 2022 test case of disappearance, possible torture, medical neglect, concealment, and rejected remedy.
- **WEB-3B-004 — Pardons accompanied by more arrests:** Personally attributable favorable initiative, sharply limited by continuing security-agency arrests.
- **WEB-3B-005 — Presidential pardons for convicted police officers:** Direct presidential action reportedly nullifying rare torture/death-in-custody convictions.
- **WEB-3B-006 — COP27 protest-call crackdown:** Nationwide preventive detention, terrorism accusations, surveillance, and temporary disappearance.
- **WEB-3B-007 — Torture-tainted mass trial:** Exemplary punishment through mass proceedings, death sentences, child abuse allegations, and denial of counsel.
- **WEB-3B-008 — Sinai executions by army-aligned forces:** Visual evidence implicating both military and aligned militias, with no reported investigation.
- **WEB-3B-009 — Reduced but continuing insurgent danger:** Genuine favorable security outcome, constrained by opaque casualty reporting and abusive counterterrorism methods.
- **WEB-3B-010 — Eritrean expulsions:** Collective refoulement, child detention, lack of asylum procedure, and exposure to disappearance or torture.
- **WEB-3B-011 — Women’s protection: enforcement and serious gaps:** Concrete FGM and harassment enforcement alongside failure to enact comprehensive violence legislation.
- **WEB-3B-012 — Religious-minority violence and mixed state response:** Identity killings persisted, but police and courts intervened in several individual cases.
- **WEB-3B-013 — LGBT targeting:** Police entrapment, forced examinations, criminalization and lack of protection against violence.
- **WEB-3B-014 — Dialogue and human-rights strategy:** Presidential reform initiatives provided releases and proposed reforms but lacked independent design and did not displace repression.
- **WEB-3B-015 — Sisi’s “people of evil” rhetoric:** Direct scapegoating language about the Muslim Brotherhood, but no located 2022 order or explicit exhortation to private violence.

## Disposition of the ten lenses

### 3B.1 — Protection from state violence

The strongest evidence is adverse. Annual monitoring found credible reports of arbitrary killing, disappearance, torture, life-threatening detention conditions, and political imprisonment, with only exceptional prosecutions (WEB-3B-002). Hadhoud’s case supplies a detailed institutional example (WEB-3B-003), while the mass trial demonstrates exemplary punishment and deficient due process (WEB-3B-007). Pardoned releases are genuine contrary evidence but were numerically and structurally limited (WEB-3B-004).

### 3B.2 — Abuse by coercive services and aligned actors

Police, National Security, prisons, military forces, and army-aligned militias were all implicated. Accountability was exceptional, and Sisi reportedly pardoned 13 convicted police officers in three custodial-death cases (WEB-3B-005). Verified Sinai footage implicated army and aligned militia members without a reported investigation (WEB-3B-008).

### 3B.3 — Personal incitement or dehumanization

Sisi publicly described the Muslim Brotherhood as “people of evil” and spoke of a “cursed alliance” of evil and destruction (WEB-3B-015). That rhetoric supported political scapegoating and blurred the distinction between armed threats and the wider opposition. However, this research did **not** locate a 2022 speech in which he explicitly ordered violence, revenge, or private attacks against a protected group or peaceful opponent. The personal-incitement finding should therefore remain narrower than the institutional-repression finding.

### 3B.4 — Due process, complaints, oversight, investigation

Formal complaint routes, prosecutors, courts, a human-rights strategy, pardon committee, and dialogue existed. In practice, prosecutors repeatedly failed to investigate security-force abuse; prisons lacked independent monitoring; emergency judgments lacked ordinary appeal; and politically protected actors enjoyed impunity (WEB-3B-002, 003, 005, 007, 014). Hadhoud’s family pursued prosecution and court review, but authorities withheld evidence and the appeal was rejected.

### 3B.5 — Emergency, terrorism, surveillance, and administrative controls

The formal emergency ended before 2022, but successor legislation preserved curfew, evacuation, military infrastructure-protection, and other exceptional powers. Terrorism accusations and surveillance were applied to suspected peaceful protest and online activity (WEB-3B-002, 006, 007, 013). This was not a pattern of narrow counterterrorism confined to armed actors.

### 3B.6 — Fear and insecurity

ISIS activity reportedly declined, a real favorable outcome (WEB-3B-009). But that improvement coexisted with nationwide fear of arrest, disappearance, surveillance, and punishment for dissent (WEB-3B-002, 006). Counterinsurgency also involved aligned-force executions (WEB-3B-008). The evidence therefore does not support treating reduced insurgent capacity as an unqualified reduction of domestic fear.

### 3B.7 — Women, children, minorities, and vulnerable groups

The record is mixed but predominantly inadequate:

- authorities prevented some FGM procedures and obtained convictions under strengthened law;
- police and courts responded to several individual sectarian killings;
- Eritrean adults and children were detained and expelled without individualized protection;
- violence against women remained widespread without comprehensive legislation;
- LGBT people faced state entrapment and abusive examinations;
- some Copts and refugees faced identity violence and barriers to protection.

See WEB-3B-010 through WEB-3B-013. Child defendants also appeared in the torture-tainted mass trial (WEB-3B-007).

### 3B.8 — Peaceful protest and organization

The November crackdown is direct evidence that authorities treated mere responsiveness to protest calls as grounds for preventive arrest and terrorism investigation. Nearly 700 detentions were documented across 18 governorates, including about 40 people whose whereabouts were initially unknown (WEB-3B-006). Civil-society investigations, bans, and retaliation also undermined the announced “year of civil society” (WEB-3B-014).

### 3B.9 — Crisis and flare-up response

Two different crisis responses matter. Against ISIS, authorities degraded the organization and investigated or prosecuted alleged attackers, but aligned forces committed apparent executions and reporting remained opaque (WEB-3B-008, 009). Against spontaneous protest calls, the response was preventive nationwide detention, surveillance, intimidation, and terrorism accusations rather than restrained crowd protection (WEB-3B-006).

### 3B.10 — Inherited-to-end safety

Compared with the beginning of 2022, ISIS attack frequency and severity reportedly declined. Some detainees were released and a limited number of vulnerable-group offenders were prosecuted. But the inherited repression apparatus remained intact: disappearance, torture, custodial deaths, political detention, protest suppression, and impunity continued, while new arrests exceeded releases in the documented comparison. There is no defensible evidence that residents as a whole ended 2022 safer from political violence and state coercion, though they may have faced a weakened insurgent threat in parts of Sinai.

## Source-state audit

### Fully extracted and accepted

The 15 WEB-3B records below.

### Opened corroboration

- **HRW, World Report 2023: Egypt:** corroborates continuing disappearance, torture, Hadhoud, Sinai executions, prison opacity, refugee expulsion, violence against women, and LGBT digital targeting. Some passages depend on the same HRW investigations used in WEB-3B-003 and WEB-3B-008.
- **USDOS, 2022 Country Report on Human Rights Practices:** independently synthesizes diplomatic reporting, NGOs, media and official material, but sometimes depends on the same local NGO incident datasets.
- **USDOS, 2022 International Religious Freedom Report:** corroborates identity killings and documents both police/court action and unresolved protection gaps.
- **CIHRS National Dialogue position paper:** corroborates that releases occurred but were regarded by domestic rights advocates as insufficient while imprisonment and arrests continued.
- **HRW World Report 2022:** establishes the inherited 2021 baseline, not a 2022 outcome.
- **OHCHR Hadhoud communication EGY 5/2022:** opened through the UN communications index, but the index page did not expose the complete communication text.

### Reused supplied sources

- E002 → WEB-3B-001, underlying constitution opened through an accessible official/constitutional reproduction.
- E003–E006 → WEB-3B-004 and WEB-3B-014; Amnesty source opened.
- E007 → WEB-3B-003; HRW/Amnesty joint investigation opened.
- E015 → WEB-3B-009; terrorism report opened.
- E036 → WEB-3B-006; HRW/ECRF source opened.
- E037 → corroboration for WEB-3B-014; CIHRS source opened.
- E046 → contextual prior only; no machine record because underlying indicator rows were not provided or reliably extracted.

### Promising but still requiring inspection

- PTS country-year raw data for Egypt, 2021 and 2022.
- CIRIGHTS Egypt country-year rows and coding notes for killing, disappearance, torture, and political imprisonment.
- V-Dem `v2x_clphy` Egypt observations and confidence intervals.
- UCDP GED and One-Sided Violence Egypt actor/event rows for 2022.
- Egyptian Public Prosecution case files for the police-officer pardons and Hadhoud investigation.
- Official pardon decrees or Gazette publication identifying the 13 police officers.
- Arabic local case reporting outside the source families already used, particularly from Sinai and Upper Egypt.
- National Council for Human Rights 2022 complaint and prison-visit records, if complete files become available.

### Rejected

- Wikipedia and Reddit results: discovery-only, unstable or user-generated.
- Generic economic-crisis reporting: rejected absent a direct physical-safety causal chain.
- Foreign-policy and overseas military material: outside Chapter 3B.
- Generic freedom or democracy scores without a specific physical-integrity nexus.
- The reported “65,000 political prisoners” figure: rejected as a precise prevalence estimate because methodology and denominator were not established.
- Municipal incidents lacking documented national policy, notice, command, or obstruction of remedy.
- UCDP nonappearance or sub-threshold cases as proof of safety: its organized-violence threshold does not capture much of the conduct at issue.

### Access blocked

- Two official State Information Service speech pages returned cache/502 failures when opened directly; their indexed text was preserved as a limited source for WEB-3B-015.
- The OHCHR Special Procedures communications search page returned an internal error on full opening.
- Exact PTS, CIRIGHTS, V-Dem and UCDP country-year rows were not exposed through accessible indexed pages.
- Some Egyptian official Facebook statements and linked leaked hospital records were unavailable or unsuitable as stable standalone records.

## Counts

Counts are by distinct URLs or dataset endpoints, not search-result appearances:

- Discovered: **39**
- Opened or substantively extracted: **22**
- Accepted into developed records: **15**
- Reused from supplied list: **11 provisional IDs consolidated into 7 accepted facts plus 1 context-only prior**
- Opened corroboration not given a separate fact record: **6**
- Rejected: **10**
- Access blocked: **6**
- Uninspected promising leads: **7**

Categories overlap where, for example, a reused source also served as an accepted source.

## Remaining questions and saturation judgment

Further research is most likely to add material information in four areas:

1. exact structured-indicator values and changes from 2021 to 2022;
2. official legal documentation of the police-officer pardons;
3. independent command-chain evidence for Sinai militias;
4. broader Arabic-language evidence on minority protection and actual remedies.

Additional general searching is unlikely to change the central findings: it mostly repeats the same detention, torture, impunity, protest, and Sinai evidence. Targeted archival or dataset work could improve precision and attribution, especially for 3B.3 and 3B.10.

## Artifact Y

 ## Orientation

The target period is calendar year 2022. Abdel Fattah el‑Sisi was president throughout. Egypt’s constitutional and administrative structure gave him substantial authority over national security policy: the president appointed the government, was supreme commander, could ratify or alter nonappealable Emergency State Security Court judgments, and possessed pardon powers. The Interior Ministry controlled the national police, Central Security Forces, and National Security Agency; the armed forces conducted North Sinai operations. The U.S. State Department assessed that civilian authorities maintained effective control over these forces. Responsibility for national policy and presidential acts is therefore relatively strong, though individual incidents cannot automatically be treated as personally ordered by Sisi. [Egyptian Constitution](https://wipolex-res.wipo.int/edocs/lexdocs/laws/en/eg/eg060en.html); [USDOS human-rights report, executive summary](https://www.ecoi.net/de/dokument/2089216.html)

The inherited baseline was already severely repressive: mass political detention, entrenched emergency and terrorism machinery, weak accountability for torture and disappearance, and an insurgency concentrated in North Sinai. Ending the nationwide state of emergency in October 2021 was favorable, but 2021 legislation retained comparable curfew, evacuation, military-assistance, and public-order powers. Emergency courts continued deciding cases initiated before expiration, with judgments not subject to ordinary appeal. [USDOS, lines 38–45 and 182–185](https://www.ecoi.net/de/dokument/2089216.html)

Non-state danger remained real. USDOS recorded at least 95 Sinai terrorist incidents and roughly 260 casualties, while judging terrorist activity lower than in earlier years. These totals were expressly uncertain because access and reporting in Sinai were restricted. The decline is material favorable evidence, but counterterrorism remained intertwined with army-backed militia abuses, extrajudicial executions, displacement, and political misuse of terrorism law. [Country Reports on Terrorism 2022](https://www.ecoi.net/en/document/2101556.html)

The principal favorable evidence is: the lower level of terrorist activity; releases of approximately 850–1,000 detainees after Sisi reactivated the Presidential Pardon Committee; closure of older prisons and transfers to newer facilities; several convictions of police personnel for earlier custodial deaths; concrete FGM prosecutions and declining reported prevalence among younger cohorts; and some minority-protection or inclusion measures.

The adverse balance is substantially broader and more directly connected to physical safety: continuing enforced disappearance and systemic torture; dangerous prison conditions and reported deaths through abuse or medical neglect; indefinite or recycled pretrial detention; presidential confirmation of nonappealable political sentences; reported presidential pardons for 13 police officers convicted in torture-related custodial deaths; retaliation against complainants and torture-video detainees; suppression surrounding COP27 protest calls; army and army-backed militia executions in Sinai; refoulement and abuse of refugees; and targeted abuse of LGBT people and Coptic protesters. The evidence supports patterns and tolerated impunity, but does not establish that Sisi personally ordered every abuse.

No adequately opened source established personal incitement by Sisi in 2022. That is a genuine gap and should not be converted into either favorable or adverse evidence.

Exact 2022 PTS, CIRIGHTS, V-Dem, and UCDP one-sided-violence observations could not be extracted from an underlying dataset in this session. Dataset landing pages and the supplied local-prior summary therefore remain contextual leads, not machine evidence.

## Compact evidence index

| WEB-3B-002 | Emergency powers survived formal emergency | Shows retained coercive legal capacity and presidential review of emergency judgments | 3B.4–3B.5, 3B.8 |
| WEB-3B-003 | Systemic torture, disappearance, and dangerous detention | Establishes recurring national pattern, not isolated incidents | 3B.1–3B.2, 3B.4–3B.6, 3B.10 |
| WEB-3B-004 | Torture-video retaliation and police impunity | Shows complaint/investigation mechanisms operating against alleged victims | 3B.2, 3B.4, 3B.8–3B.9 |
| WEB-3B-005 | Sisi pardoned convicted police officers | Direct presidential act weakening accountability | 3B.1–3B.2, 3B.4 |

| WEB-3B-009 | Sinai security outcome and remaining insurgent risk | Favorable decline plus continuing non-state violence and uncertainty | 3B.6, 3B.9–3B.10 |
| WEB-3B-010 | Army-backed militia executions | Connects aligned armed actors and army personnel to verified killings | 3B.1–3B.2, 3B.6–3B.7, 3B.9 |
| WEB-3B-011 | Refugee and LGBT targeting | Establishes targeted state abuse of vulnerable groups | 3B.1–3B.2, 3B.5, 3B.7 |
| WEB-3B-012 | Women/FGM protection and deficiencies | Preserves concrete favorable enforcement and severe remaining gaps | 3B.7, 3B.10 |
| WEB-3B-013 | Coptic protection, prosecution, and discrimination | Mixed record: prosecutions/inclusion but arbitrary detention and uneven protection | 3B.7–3B.9 |

## Ten-question disposition

1. **3B.1 — Predominantly adverse evidence.** WEB-3B-003 through 006 establish recurring disappearance, torture, arbitrary political imprisonment, custodial deaths, and weak remedies. WEB-3B-007 records meaningful but limited releases.

2. **3B.2 — Adverse institutional pattern with isolated accountability.** Police, NSA, prison, army, and army-aligned militia abuse appears in WEB-3B-003, 004, 005, 006, and 010. Three finalized police convictions are contrary evidence, but the presidential pardons and general failure to investigate weigh against treating them as a systemic remedy.

3. **3B.3 — Insufficient evidence.** No opened source established that Sisi personally incited hatred, revenge, dehumanization, or violence in 2022. Security rhetoric around terrorism existed, but the inspected material did not meet the requested personal-incitement threshold.

4. **3B.4 — Formal mechanisms, weak independence and effectiveness.** Prosecutors, courts, the NCHR, prison inspections, the pardon committee, and a human-rights strategy existed. WEB-3B-004 to 007 show retaliation, cursory investigations, delayed or unperformed inspections, nonappealable courts, and only limited releases. Some police convictions are genuine contrary evidence.

5. **3B.5 — Adverse use of security powers.** WEB-3B-002 and 008 show that ending the emergency did not end emergency-like powers; terrorism accusations, surveillance, disappearance, and preventive detention were used against peaceful critics and anticipated protesters.

6. **3B.6 — Mixed outcomes, adverse means.** Terrorist activity decreased (WEB-3B-009), but state repression, prison fear, disappearance, and militia killings persisted (WEB-3B-003, 008, 010). Reduced insurgent violence therefore did not translate cleanly into freedom from political fear.

7. **3B.7 — Mixed but substantially deficient protection.** WEB-3B-011 documents abuse of refugees and LGBT people; WEB-3B-012 documents both FGM enforcement and major domestic-violence gaps; WEB-3B-013 shows selective protection and prosecution alongside discriminatory detention and uneven Coptic access to justice.

8. **3B.8 — Strong adverse evidence.** WEB-3B-008 documents nearly 700 detentions associated with protest calls across 18 governorates, approximately 40 people missing from prosecutorial or public view, terrorism charges, surveillance, and lockdown measures. WEB-3B-013 supplies a minority-specific peaceful-protest case.

9. **3B.9 — Adverse crisis response, with counterterrorism qualification.** The COP27 response prioritized preventive repression rather than peaceful assembly (WEB-3B-008). Sinai operations degraded ISIS but included aligned-force executions without reported investigation (WEB-3B-009–010).

10. **3B.10 — No defensible finding of overall safety improvement from this one-year window.** Non-state terrorism declined, and several protective measures were real. Yet widespread state abuse, impunity, political detention, and fear remained. Exact inherited-to-end PTS/CIRIGHTS/V-Dem/UCDP series and independently confirmed casualty denominators remain missing, preventing a stronger aggregate conclusion.

## Corroboration and source disposition

**Independent corroboration**

- HRW’s annual account independently corroborates the USDOS pattern findings on NSA disappearance, torture, prison abuse, political detention, and Sinai militia executions.
- Amnesty and HRW jointly investigated Hadhoud using family and lawyer interviews, hospital sources, leaked medical records, and official correspondence.
- HRW’s COP27 detention account relied on ECRF case documentation and was consistent with Amnesty’s broader account of simultaneous releases and new arrests.
- USDOS terrorism reporting combined government releases and media reporting but expressly disclaimed confirmed casualty totals.
- USDOS religious-freedom reporting preserves both government protective actions and civil-society criticism.

**Reused and reopened supplied sources**

- E002 → WEB-3B-001.
- E003/E005/E006 were reopened; their distinct facts are represented primarily in WEB-3B-007 and contextualized against WEB-3B-003/008.
- E007 → WEB-3B-006.
- E015 → WEB-3B-009.
- E036 → WEB-3B-008.
- E046 remains context only because the underlying local dataset was not inspectable.

**Uninspected leads**

- PTS country-year download.
- CIRIGHTS country-year download.
- V-Dem 2022 physical-violence observations.
- UCDP one-sided-violence event rows for Egypt.
- The underlying Egyptian Front/Freedom Initiative sexual-violence report.
- Full official Presidential Pardon Committee name-level release list.
- Official pardon decrees for the 13 police officers.
- Egyptian official National Human Rights Strategy implementation report.

**Rejected**

- Wikipedia, Reddit, and search snippets as evidence.
- Economic hardship claims without a specific physical-safety causal chain.
- Foreign or overseas conduct without a demonstrated domestic nexus.
- Pre-2022 incidents except where necessary to identify an inherited pattern or the prior offense underlying a 2022 judgment/pardon.

**Blocked/partial**

- The WIPO constitutional HTML/PDF open returned an internal retrieval error; its stable text page had been indexed, while authority was independently supported by USDOS.
- Dataset landing pages for V-Dem/CIRIGHTS were inspectable only at metadata level, not country-year observations.
- No local workspace source could be opened because the session lacked workspace-shell access.

Additional general NGO annual reports would now mostly duplicate established patterns.
