# Félix Tshisekedi — 2B (Congo, The Democratic Republic of the)


## Artifact X

 ## Scope and orientation

Period assessed: **1 January–31 December 2022**. “2022–2022” is treated as a single calendar year. No score is assigned.

### Authority

Félix Tshisekedi was president throughout 2022. Constitutionally and practically he was the supreme commander of the armed forces and police, chaired the Higher Defence Council, appointed senior security officials, directed foreign policy, and controlled the presidential decrees sustaining the state of siege. A formal declaration of war nevertheless required cabinet deliberation, Defence Council advice, and parliamentary authorization. Parliament repeatedly approved two-week extensions of the state of siege, so responsibility was shared but presidentially initiated and sustained.

Tshisekedi directly participated in the Luanda interstate negotiations and authorized the Nairobi process, Ugandan operations on Congolese territory, and deployment of the East African Community Regional Force. Weak command systems, fragmented FARDC units, provincial military government, foreign partners, MONUSCO, and autonomous or semi-coordinated militias substantially limited practical control over individual operations.

### Baseline and conflict gate

The international-security exposure gate is decisively met. Tshisekedi inherited:

- Decades of regionalized warfare in eastern DRC, involving Rwanda, Uganda, Burundi, foreign armed groups, and numerous Congolese militias.
- A 2021 state of siege in North Kivu and Ituri.
- Operation Shujaa, the joint FARDC–Ugandan campaign against the ADF, begun in late 2021.
- M23’s reactivation in November 2021 and unresolved obligations arising from earlier peace arrangements.
- Extremely weak civilian protection and military accountability.

The local D13/UCDP prior records conflict events, fatalities, one-sided violence and government-actor killings but does not expose its numerical rows in the supplied material. An opened UCDP-authored publication provides the strongest accessible D13 fact: **at least 3,200 civilians were killed in one-sided violence in DRC during 2022**, while expressly warning that this excludes crossfire and indiscriminate shelling. UCDP classified Rwandan support to M23 against the Congolese government as one of five 2022 cases of state support to a rebel organization fighting another state.

The D14/SIPRI prior and SIPRI-derived series report approximately **US$371 million** in DRC military expenditure in 2022, about **21.2% above 2021**. This measures spending, not procurement integrity or ruler enrichment.

### Shocks and constraints

The major period shock was the sharp M23 offensive, including the capture of Bunagana and other territory, with increasingly strong evidence of direct Rwandan military intervention. The ADF, CODECO, Nyatura and other inherited threats continued simultaneously. Conflict displaced hundreds of thousands of people, while humanitarian agencies operated under severe access and funding constraints. The Ukraine-related food and energy shock added fiscal and humanitarian pressure but does not explain the core military decisions.

Reporting was comparatively plural but incomplete. MONUSCO, UN experts, NGOs, regional bodies and local journalists documented major incidents, yet military-site restrictions, insecurity, intimidation and inaccessible M23-held territory produced substantial undercounting and attribution uncertainty.

## Compact evidence index

| ID | Fact | Why it matters |
|---|---|---|
| WEB-2B-001 | Constitutional and practical command | Establishes Tshisekedi’s authority and institutional constraints. |
| WEB-2B-002 | UCDP conflict burden | Establishes unusually severe conflict exposure and deterioration. |
| WEB-2B-003 | M23/Rwandan intervention | Corroborates a genuine external defensive threat, while recording contested shelling allegations. |
| WEB-2B-004 | Luanda ceasefire framework | Direct evidence that Tshisekedi accepted ceasefire, withdrawal, verification and dialogue. |
| WEB-2B-005 | Nairobi dialogue commitments | Shows political, humanitarian and DDR alternatives to force. |
| WEB-2B-006 | Partial implementation, continued fighting | Prevents treating signed commitments as a completed settlement. |
| WEB-2B-007 | FARDC collaboration with FDLR/armed groups | Central adverse proxy-control and lawful-security evidence. |
| WEB-2B-008 | State-force civilian harm | Documents FARDC killings and sexual violence, with partial accountability measures. |
| WEB-2B-009 | State-of-siege failure and abuse | Connects presidential emergency policy to repression and worsened protection outcomes. |
| WEB-2B-010 | Authorized foreign/regional forces | Shows reliance on Uganda, MONUSCO and EACRF against genuine threats, with control risks. |
| WEB-2B-011 | Public threat framing and restraint | Records substantially corroborated Rwanda claims, coercive rhetoric, diplomacy and an anti-stigmatization instruction. |
| WEB-2B-012 | Military expenditure increase | Establishes mobilization but not patronage or personal enrichment. |
| WEB-2B-013 | Year-end end state | Shows limited diplomatic progress but greater regionalization, displacement and insecurity. |

## Disposition of the ten lenses

### 2B.1 — Diplomacy versus force

**Mixed, with meaningful favorable evidence.** Tshisekedi joined the Luanda and Nairobi processes, signed the 23 November ceasefire framework, accepted monitoring and withdrawal arrangements, and supported DDR and humanitarian commitments (WEB-2B-004, 005). He also continued Uganda’s Operation Shujaa, sought a force-capable EAC deployment, strengthened FARDC “strike capacity,” and excluded direct M23 bargaining unless withdrawal conditions were met (WEB-2B-010, 011). The underlying M23/Rwandan and ADF threats were credible, so military resistance was not simply a war of choice.

### 2B.2 — War of choice, cross-border coercion or proxy conflict

**No persuasive evidence that Tshisekedi initiated an annexationist or external war of choice.** The 2022 M23/RDF offensive was directed against Congolese territory (WEB-2B-003). However, DRC authorized Ugandan forces to operate on its territory and FARDC elements cooperated with FDLR and other armed groups, extending regional proxy dynamics and creating foreseeable escalation risks (WEB-2B-007, 010). The available evidence does not establish that Tshisekedi personally ordered the FDLR cooperation.

### 2B.3 — Defensive need versus prestige or manufactured threats

**The central external threat was genuine, but security policy was not cleanly confined to lawful defense.** UN, EU, UCDP and independent reporting corroborated RDF support for M23 (WEB-2B-002, 003, 007). Tshisekedi’s description of Rwandan aggression therefore was not manufactured. Calling M23 “terrorists,” expanding military capacity, and relying on abusive militias nevertheless simplified a conflict containing unresolved political, communal and prior-agreement issues (WEB-2B-005, 007, 011). Evidence for diversionary or personal-prestige motivation is insufficient.

### 2B.4 — Civilian protection and humanitarian law

**Adverse overall, with limited remedial evidence.** Armed groups caused most documented abuses, but FARDC personnel committed extrajudicial killings and conflict-related sexual violence, while state-of-siege authorities killed protesters and subjected civilians to military courts (WEB-2B-008, 009). Some soldiers and police were convicted, and action plans and training were initiated, but impunity—especially at higher levels—remained pervasive. Evidence tying individual battlefield violations directly to Tshisekedi is generally absent.

### 2B.5 — Control of allies, proxies and arms recipients

**Serious adverse evidence.** UN findings and the EU’s year-end response identified FARDC cooperation with FDLR and other Congolese armed groups, despite their abusive records. Arms-trafficking charges against FARDC officers and abuses by allied Burundian actors reinforce the control problem (WEB-2B-007). The Luanda commitment to end all support to armed groups and some arrests count as contrary evidence, but implementation was incomplete. Direct presidential authorization of particular militia cooperation remains unproved.

### 2B.6 — Truthful public explanation

**The principal accusation was substantially corroborated, but official framing was selective.** Independent UN evidence supported the claim that Rwanda materially backed M23 and operated inside DRC (WEB-2B-003, 011). Official descriptions omitted or downplayed FARDC cooperation with FDLR, which provided Rwanda with a real—though not legally sufficient—security grievance (WEB-2B-007). There is no adequate evidence of fabricated intelligence directed by Tshisekedi.

### 2B.7 — Ceasefires, talks and reconciliation

**Favorable at the level of presidential commitment; weak and incomplete in outcome.** Tshisekedi signed or accepted the Luanda ceasefire, bilateral normalization, monitoring, withdrawal, cantonment, refugee-return and cessation-of-support provisions. The Nairobi dialogue added prisoner release, child-soldier release, humanitarian access, consultations and P-DDRCS review (WEB-2B-004, 005). M23 withdrew from Kibumba, but continued hostilities and incomplete compliance showed that the process had not produced peace by year’s end (WEB-2B-006).

### 2B.8 — Military spending and patronage

**Real security need is established; patronage conclusion is not.** SIPRI-derived data indicate spending rose to about US$371 million, 21.2% above 2021, during an exceptionally severe conflict year (WEB-2B-002, 012). Weak payroll and procurement controls make diversion plausible, but the searched 2022 material does not establish that the increase enriched Tshisekedi, his family, or a specified military network. This lens remains low-confidence.

### 2B.9 — Accountability and correction

**Partial institutional accountability, little ruler-level accountability.** Authorities convicted some soldiers, police and intelligence personnel and arrested officers accused of supplying CODECO; joint UN action plans and training were adopted (WEB-2B-007, 008). But the government repeatedly extended a demonstrably ineffective state of siege, did not provide an effective public remedy for its structural abuses, and higher-level impunity persisted (WEB-2B-009). No evidence was found of Tshisekedi accepting personal responsibility for failed protection or FARDC–FDLR cooperation.

### 2B.10 — End-state change

**Relations and security were worse at the end of 2022 than at its beginning, despite credible diplomacy.** The year ended with M23 holding territory, direct Rwandan involvement, expanded regional-force deployments, severed ambassadorial relations, mass displacement and continued fighting. Luanda and Nairobi created a lawful diplomatic architecture and produced one partial withdrawal, but not a durable settlement (WEB-2B-004, 005, 006, 013). Much deterioration was caused by external and inherited actors rather than initiated by Tshisekedi.

## Source-state audit

### Opened independent corroboration

- UN Secretary-General/MONUSCO reports S/2022/503 and related 2022 reporting corroborate the multiple-actor threat, joint Ugandan operations, FARDC violations and limited prosecutions.
- Human Rights Watch’s M23 investigation independently corroborates M23 civilian killings and reviews UN photographic/aerial evidence of RDF personnel.
- The EU’s 31 December statement independently accepts the UN experts’ findings concerning both RDF/M23 support and FARDC–FDLR cooperation.
- UCDP’s peer-reviewed 2022 review independently classifies Rwandan support to M23 and quantifies DRC one-sided violence.
- The U.S. country report is secondary corroboration substantially dependent on UNJHRO, UN experts and NGO reporting; it is not counted as independent where it repeats those sources.

### Reused supplied sources, opened before use

E006, E008/E057, E009, E010, E025/E056, E026, E045, E054 and E055 were reconsidered. E054, E055, E006, E045 and E057 were opened and used. E025/E056 refer to the same Kenya Ministry of Defence article and were deduplicated. E008 and E057 are the same U.S. report and were deduplicated.

### Promising leads still requiring inspection

- Full French/English pagination of S/2022/967, especially annexes identifying individual FARDC units, commanders, dates and supplied weapons.
- National Assembly extension votes and Defence Committee reports for responsibility shared between the presidency and legislature.
- Audited 2022 defence appropriations, procurement contracts, payroll and off-budget security spending.
- Full Operation Shujaa agreement, rules of engagement and civilian-casualty investigations.
- Presidential correspondence or orders addressing FARDC–FDLR cooperation after UN findings.
- Implementation records for Nairobi prisoner-release, humanitarian-access and P-DDRCS commitments.

### Rejected

- Wikipedia conflict chronologies: useful for discovery but superseded by UN, PA-X, official and NGO records.
- Macrotrends and Helgi as final SIPRI authorities: they reproduce SIPRI data but are not the underlying database.
- Rwanda’s later UNSC statements: useful contrary advocacy but outside the target year and not independent evidence of 2022 events.
- 2024–2026 reporting on later Wazalendo structures: potentially retrospective, but too remote to establish Tshisekedi’s 2022 knowledge or orders.
- Government white-paper allegations not independently verified were not used as proof of individual events.

### Access-blocked or technically incomplete

- Local D13/D14 underlying files: the workspace supplied only the earlier summary, and no shell/file-reading interface was available in this research session.
- UN S/2022/967 PDF: search extraction exposed key text, but the direct UN PDF open returned an internal error.
- African Union communiqué URL: direct open was rejected by the retrieval service’s URL-safety layer.
- UN Secretary-General 25 November statement: direct open returned a cache miss.
- Leganet constitution page and SIPRI 2022 fact-sheet landing page: direct retrieval failed or timed out; search extraction and independent official/academic records were used cautiously.

### Counts

Counting unique underlying publications rather than duplicate URLs:

- Sources/leads discovered: **38**
- Successfully opened or substantively extracted: **21**
- Accepted as evidence or independent corroboration: **17**
- Supplied sources reused after opening: **9 provisional IDs**, representing **7 unique publications**
- Rejected as final evidence: **7**
- Access-blocked or technically incomplete: **5**
- Uninspected promising leads retained: **6**

## Remaining research value

Further searching is most likely to add material information on:

1. Who authorized FARDC–FDLR cooperation and when the presidency learned of it.
2. Rules of engagement and civilian-harm reviews for Operation Shujaa and FARDC artillery.
3. Defence procurement, payroll diversion and patronage.
4. Implementation of prisoner-release, humanitarian and DDR commitments.
5. Ruler-level acceptance or avoidance of accountability.

The factual landscape for the conflict baseline, RDF/M23 intervention, peace-process commitments, state-of-siege abuses and year-end deterioration is comparatively saturated. The military-patronage and ruler-direction questions are not.

## Artifact Y

 ## Orientation

The exposure gate is clearly met. In 2022, the DRC remained immersed in inherited armed conflicts in the east, while M23’s renewed offensive—independently associated with Rwandan military support—gave the crisis a major cross-border dimension. Other armed groups, Ugandan and Burundian operations, MONUSCO, and weak state control further constrained Tshisekedi’s choices.

Tshisekedi possessed substantial but not exclusive authority. Constitutionally, he was commander-in-chief, chaired the Superior Defence Council, appointed senior commanders jointly with government institutions, could declare a state of siege, and shared defence, security, and foreign policy with the government. Actual civilian control remained limited.

The favorable evidence is substantial on diplomacy: Tshisekedi participated in the Nairobi and Luanda processes, accepted a ceasefire and monitored M23 withdrawal, continued political consultations, DDR review, humanitarian access, prisoner screening, and regional verification. No reviewed evidence shows that he initiated an interstate war, sought annexation, or treated force as the sole first option.

The adverse evidence is strongest on conduct and control. Tshisekedi prolonged an inherited state of siege whose stated civilian-protection objective was not achieved; military authorities suppressed critics and tried civilians. FARDC personnel committed killings, conflict-related sexual violence, attacks on schools, resource predation, and collaborated with armed groups, including documented cooperation with the FDLR during the M23 conflict. Accountability existed at lower levels but remained selective and weak at senior levels.

The net end-state was worse: conflict deaths, displacement, M23 territorial advances, and interstate tension increased during 2022. That deterioration cannot be attributed solely to Tshisekedi because M23 and Rwanda were major independent causal actors and the underlying conflicts predated him. Evidence concerning his personal knowledge of particular atrocities, military procurement patronage, or deliberate public misinformation remains insufficient.

The D13/UCDP local prior was supplied as recording conflict events, fatalities, one-sided violence, and government-actor killings. The D14/SIPRI prior was also identified, but its country-year cells could not be independently opened here; the current SIPRI database confirms its definitions, annual revision, and open-source methodology, not the supplied numeric value.

## Ten-question disposition

1. **2B.1 — Mixed, with meaningful diplomatic evidence.** Records 003–005 show a genuine external threat alongside Tshisekedi’s participation in two negotiated processes. Record 006 shows prolonged reliance on emergency military rule despite serious evidence of ineffectiveness and abuse.

2. **2B.2 — No demonstrated war of choice or annexation; adverse proxy evidence.** The reviewed record does not show Tshisekedi initiating an interstate war. Record 007 nevertheless shows FARDC cooperation with armed groups during the M23 conflict, including FDLR cooperation independently documented by UN experts. Personal presidential authorization is not established.

3. **2B.3 — The central M23/Rwanda threat was genuine, not manufactured.** Record 003 independently corroborates M23 atrocities and Rwandan involvement. Record 006 weakens the justification for indefinitely maintaining the state of siege because deaths increased and the promised security result did not materialize. Prestige, diversion, or revenge motives were not demonstrated.

4. **2B.4 — Adverse overall, with limited corrective measures.** Records 006 and 008 document unlawful killings, sexual violence, military prosecution of civilians, displacement, and abuse. Records 008–009 document action plans, UN-supported training, investigations, and some convictions, but these did not overcome persistent impunity.

5. **2B.5 — Material failure to control forces and armed-group partners.** Record 007 documents FARDC collaboration with armed groups and alleged arms transfers; record 008 documents state-agent abuse. Record 004 shows that Tshisekedi later accepted an explicit regional obligation to end all support to armed groups. Evidence of effective implementation in 2022 is weak.

6. **2B.6 — Official threat claims were substantially corroborated, but the state-of-siege narrative became misleading by omission.** Record 003 supports the government’s core claim about M23/Rwandan involvement. Record 006 shows that authorities continued presenting emergency rule as protective despite worsening deaths and repression. No direct evidence establishes fabricated intelligence ordered by Tshisekedi.

7. **2B.7 — Favorable and comparatively well documented.** Records 004–005 establish participation in ceasefire, withdrawal, verification, consultation, DDR, prisoner-release, child-release, and humanitarian-access arrangements. Implementation was incomplete, and the Luanda framework retained coercive force as a fallback.

8. **2B.8 — Insufficient for a firm patronage finding.** Record 010 establishes the authoritative SIPRI measurement source, while records 007–008 reveal resource predation and weak control within FARDC. The reviewed evidence does not trace 2022 procurement, off-budget expenditure, contracts, beneficiaries, or Tshisekedi-directed enrichment. This lens remains materially underdetermined.

9. **2B.9 — Partial lower-level accountability, little demonstrated presidential accountability.** Record 009 records prosecutions and convictions but widespread impunity, especially at higher ranks. Record 006 contains no acknowledgment or correction by Tshisekedi commensurate with the state of siege’s failed protection rationale. Personal obstruction was not established.

10. **2B.10 — Relations and security deteriorated, with substantial exogenous causation.** M23 resurgence, Rwandan intervention, civilian deaths, displacement, and interstate hostility made the end-of-2022 environment less peaceful than the inherited start. Records 003–005 show Tshisekedi attempting regional containment, so deterioration alone is not proof that he caused it.

## Source disposition

**Independent corroboration**

- M23 civilian abuses and reported Rwandan involvement: HRW eyewitness investigation corroborated by the UN Group of Experts and the regional verification mechanism.
- FARDC–armed-group cooperation: UN Group of Experts corroborated in the U.S. annual report; arrests of five FARDC officers provide a distinct domestic enforcement indicator.
- State-of-siege harms: Amnesty’s interviews and document review corroborate HRW and UNJHRO reporting.
- Peace commitments: PA-X reproduces the Luanda and Nairobi instruments; their institutional provenance is distinct from rights-monitoring sources.

**Reused and reopened**

- E006 → WEB-2B-006.
- E045 → WEB-2B-003.
- E054 → WEB-2B-004.
- E055 → WEB-2B-005.
- E057 → WEB-2B-002, WEB-2B-007, WEB-2B-008, and WEB-2B-009, with separate precise locators and shared-source dependence.
- The Senate-hosted Constitution and SIPRI database were newly opened.

**Uninspected leads**

- E005, local D13/D14 structured prior: supplied summary available, underlying local tables unavailable to the browsing interface.
- E009, AU Peace and Security Council communiqué: official page returned an internal-access error.
- E010, UN Secretary-General statement: official page returned an internal-access error.
- E025/E056, EACRF withdrawal report: not needed as an accepted atomic record after PA-X and UN-process evidence established the relevant commitments; potentially useful for implementation.
- E046, Congolese white paper: retained only as a belligerent-government lead because the central Rwanda/M23 fact was independently established.
- E026, Congo Research Group summary: useful secondary map to the UN report but superseded by stronger underlying evidence.
- UCDP event-level country-year tables remain the principal missing quantitative extraction.

**Rejected**

- Wikipedia, Reddit, generic fact aggregators, and unattributed military-spending sites were excluded.
- The Congolese white paper was not used to independently prove Rwandan aggression.
- Purely domestic protest repression was used only where directly tied to international-conflict emergency governance, not as an independent 2B aggression finding.

**Blocked**

- UN S/2022/967 PDF landing page opened, but text extraction repeatedly failed; its pertinent conclusions were accepted only where independently reproduced or corroborated by fully opened sources.
- World Bank API calls for the 2022 SIPRI-derived DRC cells were rejected by the browsing safety layer.
- AU and UN statement URLs above returned internal errors.

## Discovery and disposition counts

- Candidate source units considered: 25.
- Fully opened underlying sources: 8.
- Accepted atomic records: 10.
- Accepted records based on reopened supplied sources: 7.
- Independent source families represented in accepted records: 7.
- Uninspected or superseded leads: 7.
- Blocked access attempts: 4.
- Rejected low-quality or non-independent source categories: 4.
- Accepted records resting on the same U.S. report: 4; their shared dependence is explicitly marked.

## Remaining research value

Further research is most likely to add material information for:

- **2B.8:** the exact D14/SIPRI 2022 value, defence-budget execution, emergency or classified accounts, procurement contracts, and beneficiary networks.
- **2B.5/2B.9:** command-level knowledge of FARDC–FDLR cooperation and final dispositions of cases against officers.
- **2B.6:** Tshisekedi’s speeches and internal briefing record concerning Rwanda, M23, and continuation of the state of siege.
- **2B.10:** event-level UCDP comparison between the beginning and end of 2022 and verified implementation of the November ceasefire.

The central conclusions on exposure, genuine external threat, diplomatic engagement, state-of-siege failure, serious FARDC-control problems, and incomplete accountability are already supported by convergent evidence. Additional general searching is now more likely to repeat those findings than change them.

# Xi Jinping — 1B (China)


## Artifact X

 ## Scope and bottom line

Period resolved as **1 January–31 December 2022**; the user’s “2022–2022” is treated as a single target year.

**Exposure classification: `material_exposure`.** Xi Jinping chaired the Chinese Communist Party, the state, and the Central Military Commission (CMC). Public scholarship indicates that alerting or launching Chinese nuclear forces required agreement at the highest party-military level, while Xi’s October 2022 report directed implementation of the “CMC chairman responsibility system,” expansion of “strategic deterrence,” and modernization of joint command, warning, cyber, and intelligent warfare capabilities.

The record is mixed but not symmetrical:

- China maintained its longstanding declaratory no-first-use (NFU) policy, joined the January P5 risk-reduction statement, promoted safeguards and nuclear-security cooperation, and Xi personally opposed nuclear use and threats amid Russia’s war.
- China did not issue direct nuclear threats in the Taiwan crisis.
- Against that, China was undertaking the largest expansion in its nuclear history, building more than 300 apparent missile silos and increasing readiness, diversity, and potential launch-on-warning capability. Its operational stockpile exceeded 400 warheads by the Pentagon’s 2022 estimate.
- During the August Taiwan crisis, China conducted unprecedented coercive exercises and suspended senior military and maritime-safety dialogue—the channels most relevant to controlling accidental escalation.
- China vetoed strengthened UN measures against North Korea after renewed ICBM testing, although it argued that sanctions were counterproductive and harmful to diplomacy and civilians.
- Formal nuclear-safety and military-AI rules were precautionary, but public evidence does not independently establish implementation inside the opaque nuclear command system.

This dossier describes evidence and does **not** assign a score.

## Orientation

### Authority

Xi’s personal authority was exceptionally broad. He was CCP general secretary and CMC chairman throughout 2022, and president of the PRC. His Party Congress report explicitly required stronger implementation of the CMC-chairman responsibility system and a stronger strategic-deterrence system. Independent analysis assessed that major nuclear-force changes were highly unlikely without Xi’s authorization, although operational records and specific orders remain secret.

Attribution is therefore strongest for Xi’s direct speeches, Party-Congress directives, leadership-level diplomacy, and broad force-development policy. It is weaker for technical implementation, individual PLA deployments, regulatory enforcement, and ambassadorial statements.

### Inherited baseline

China entered 2022 with:

- a longstanding official NFU doctrine and negative security assurances;
- a historically small, centrally controlled, low-readiness arsenal, with many warheads normally separated from launchers;
- an arsenal already undergoing rapid transformation since approximately 2019;
- three large apparent ICBM-silo fields already under construction;
- the NPT, IAEA safeguards, an unratified CTBT signature, a nuclear-safety statute effective since 2018, and participation in P5 risk-reduction diplomacy.

The silo expansion therefore was not initiated wholly inside calendar 2022, but Xi’s government maintained and developed it during the target year.

### Shocks and constraints

The central shocks were Russia’s invasion of Ukraine and nuclear threats; North Korean missile and nuclear developments; deteriorating US-China strategic relations; and the August Taiwan crisis after Nancy Pelosi’s visit. These created unusually discriminating opportunities for restraint, crisis communication, proliferation policy, and nuclear rhetoric.

### Information environment

China publishes doctrine and legal instruments but not a warhead total, fissile-material production data, alert records, nuclear exercises, incident reports, or detailed command arrangements. Independent assessment consequently relies heavily on satellite imagery and external intelligence estimates. Absence of public accidents, dissent, or nuclear threats is not proof of safety or restraint.

The workspace shell was unavailable, so the requested local researcher guide and local D17/FAS files could not be opened. Supplied local facts were used only after their underlying web sources were opened wherever accessible.

## Compact evidence index

| ID | Fact | Why it matters |
|---|---|---|
| WEB-1B-001 | Xi’s concentrated nuclear and military authority | Establishes practical attribution for doctrine and major force changes. |
| WEB-1B-002 | Inherited NFU, centralized custody, low readiness | Provides the inherited risk-control baseline. |
| WEB-1B-003 | More than 300 new apparent missile silos | Strong evidence of rapid quantitative and qualitative expansion. |
| WEB-1B-004 | Stockpile exceeded 400 operational warheads | Shows force growth during Xi’s tenure and target-year posture. |
| WEB-1B-005 | China joined the January P5 risk statement | Favorable formal commitment on nuclear war, arms races, and unauthorized use. |
| WEB-1B-006 | China reaffirmed NFU and risk reduction at the NPT | Favorable doctrine and diplomacy, limited by lack of verification and force expansion. |
| WEB-1B-007 | Nuclear Safety Law framework | Institutional evidence on custody, regulation, expertise, emergency response, and transparency. |
| WEB-1B-008 | Xi opposed nuclear use and threats over Ukraine | Direct ruler-attributed responsible rhetoric in a live nuclear crisis. |
| WEB-1B-009 | Taiwan exercises plus suspension of safety dialogue | Adverse crisis-management and coercion evidence. |
| WEB-1B-010 | China vetoed additional DPRK sanctions | Adverse or disputed proliferation response involving a client-dependent state. |
| WEB-1B-011 | Military-AI precautionary position | Favorable formal dual-use governance principles, without verified implementation. |
| WEB-1B-012 | Xi ordered stronger deterrence, warning, cyber and intelligent forces | Links risk institutions with simultaneous acceleration of strategic capability. |
| WEB-1B-013 | CTBT cooperation without ratification | Mixed arms-control record: monitoring support but no binding ratification. |
| WEB-1B-014 | China supported IAEA safeguards and peaceful-use cooperation | Favorable multilateral position, principally declaratory. |

## Disposition by lens

1. **1B.1 — Risk reduction versus leverage:** Mixed, with substantial adverse evidence. WEB-1B-005, 006 and 008 show risk-reduction commitments; WEB-1B-003, 004 and 012 show rapid expansion. WEB-1B-009 shows conventional coercion and withdrawal of guardrails during crisis.

2. **1B.2 — Nuclear rhetoric:** Substantially favorable in the observable record. China retained NFU, joined the P5 statement, and Xi opposed nuclear use and threats (WEB-1B-005, 006, 008). No direct Chinese nuclear-use threat was found in 2022. This does not resolve whether nuclear capability was implicitly supporting Taiwan coercion.

3. **1B.3 — Command, custody and accident prevention:** Mixed and opaque. Centralized civilian authorization, separated warheads, and the statutory safety framework are favorable (WEB-1B-002, 007). Increasing readiness, possible launch-on-warning development, entanglement with conventional systems, and secrecy create countervailing risks (WEB-1B-003, 004, 012).

4. **1B.4 — Arms control and inspection:** Mixed. China supported the NPT, IAEA safeguards, CTBT objectives, fissile-material negotiations, and P5 dialogue (WEB-1B-005, 006, 013, 014). It had not ratified the CTBT, rejected the TPNW approach, did not enter a binding arsenal-control arrangement, and expanded its forces.

5. **1B.5 — Nuclear shield for conventional coercion:** Important adverse possibility, not conclusively proven. The Taiwan exercises and strategic-deterrence buildup coincide (WEB-1B-009, 012), and independent scholarship identified a possible “nuclear shield” for non-nuclear escalation. China did not explicitly threaten nuclear use, and there is insufficient evidence that Xi ordered the exercises under nuclear cover.

6. **1B.6 — Proliferation by allies or clients:** Adverse/disputed. China vetoed additional DPRK sanctions after ICBM tests (WEB-1B-010). China argued sanctions would worsen humanitarian conditions and reduce diplomatic prospects; the record does not establish Chinese assistance to the DPRK nuclear program in 2022.

7. **1B.7 — Expertise and institutions:** Limited favorable institutional evidence. The Nuclear Safety Law mandates qualified specialists, independent regulation, training, research, inspections, and emergency capacity (WEB-1B-007); Xi called for strengthened risk-monitoring and technical systems (WEB-1B-012). No sufficiently independent evidence was found about nuclear advisers’ professional autonomy versus political loyalty.

8. **1B.8 — Crisis behavior and channels:** Mixed but materially adverse in the Taiwan crisis. Xi’s Ukraine rhetoric was de-escalatory (WEB-1B-008), but Beijing suspended senior military and maritime-security dialogue while conducting unprecedented Taiwan exercises (WEB-1B-009).

9. **1B.9 — Other catastrophic technologies:** Formally favorable but implementation-uncertain. China’s military-AI paper required human control, suspension capability, accountability, lifecycle safeguards, and restraint against premature deployment (WEB-1B-011). Xi ordered stronger cyber, biological, nuclear, data, warning, and emergency systems while also accelerating intelligent and cyber warfare capability (WEB-1B-012). No independently verifiable 2022 outcome evidence was found for biological or frontier-AI safeguards.

10. **1B.10 — End-of-period posture:** More dangerous in capability and crisis-interaction terms than the inherited Xi-era baseline, despite retained doctrinal restraints. Force size, silo capacity, readiness options, and Taiwan escalation all increased; NFU, centralized authorization, safety law, and responsible rhetoric remained mitigating features (WEB-1B-002–009, 012).

## Source-state accounting

### Opened corroboration

- Arms Control Association’s 2023 synthesis corroborates centralized control, NFU continuity, low inherited readiness, force expansion, and the uncertainty surrounding Xi’s motives.
- SIPRI independently corroborates the silo buildout and broad expansion.
- Pentagon reporting independently estimates more than 400 operational warheads, but is an adversarial-government source and depends partly on classified intelligence.
- Reuters independently documents the Taiwan exercises and suspension of dialogue.
- UN meeting records corroborate the DPRK veto and record both China’s justification and other members’ objections.
- CTBTO records corroborate Chinese monitoring-station cooperation and continued non-ratification.

### Reused supplied sources, opened before use

E001, E002, E034, E035, E036, E039, E040, E042, E046 and E048. E003 was not reused because the supplied Carnegie URL concerned South Asia and did not cleanly support the China-specific claim as described. E037, E041, E044, E045 and E047 remain useful leads but were not needed for a distinct accepted record here.

### Promising leads still needing inspection

- Full 2022 Pentagon China Military Power Report PDF, especially its nuclear command, launch-on-warning, and fissile-material sections.
- FAS *Chinese Nuclear Weapons, 2023* / later Nuclear Notebook tables for precise end-2022 estimates.
- Tong Zhao’s October 2022 APLN paper on Russian coercive signaling lessons.
- Carnegie’s 2022 strategic-stability study on conventional/nuclear/cyber entanglement.
- IAEA annual safeguards and nuclear-security implementation records specific to China.
- Chinese-language CMC regulations governing Rocket Force custody, authentication, and accident reporting.
- Personnel records for 2022 nuclear command, CAEA, NNSA, and Rocket Force appointments.

### Rejected

- Wikipedia P5 statement: rejected because the MFA primary text was available.
- Supplied Carnegie South Asia URL (E003): rejected for claim/URL mismatch.
- Reddit summaries: rejected as derivative and unnecessary.
- Later 2024–26 Chinese white papers: rejected as primary evidence for 2022, except as possible retrospective leads.
- Search snippets alleging implementation without a directly inspectable underlying record: rejected.

### Access blocked or technically incomplete

- Local researcher guide and D17/FAS workspace files: shell interface unavailable.
- UN press page for the DPRK vote timed out on direct open; its search extraction and separate UN records were available.
- Full Pentagon PDF was discovered but not fully extracted in this pass; the official release and official detailed summary were opened.

## Remaining research value

Further research is most likely to add material information to lenses **1B.3, 1B.5, 1B.7, and 1B.9**—especially launch-on-warning implementation, nuclear-conventional entanglement in the Taiwan theater, appointment criteria and professional autonomy, and actual AI/biosecurity enforcement. Open-source searching is otherwise approaching repetition. Classified or Chinese internal records would be far more discriminating than additional general commentary.

## Artifact Y

 ## Orientation

Period resolved as calendar year **2022**. Exposure classification: **material_exposure**.

Xi Jinping possessed exceptionally strong authority-based responsibility. He was CCP general secretary, PRC president, and chairman of both Central Military Commissions; his October report reaffirmed the “CMC chairman responsibility system,” absolute Party control of the PLA, centralized national-security leadership, and expansion of “strategic deterrence.” Public evidence does not reveal the precise nuclear-release procedure, but major changes to force structure and doctrine were unlikely to proceed without his approval.

The inherited baseline combined a comparatively small arsenal, a longstanding declaratory no-first-use policy, reportedly low peacetime readiness and centralized custody, with modernization already underway. In 2022, the decisive adverse development was independently observed rapid expansion: more than 300 new missile silos and a U.S. estimate exceeding 400 operational warheads. The expansion was opaque, outside any negotiated numerical limit or inspection arrangement, and accompanied by work on survivability, early warning and broader strategic-deterrence systems.

Favorable evidence includes China’s participation in the January P5 risk-reduction statement; continued no-first-use and negative-security-assurance declarations; support for the NPT, CTBT, safeguards, JCPOA restoration and human control of military AI; an established civil nuclear-safety statute; and IAEA verification at selected Chinese facilities. These were primarily declaratory, legal or limited-scope institutional facts rather than demonstrated restraint of the weapons buildup.

The clearest 2022 crisis evidence is adverse: during the Taiwan crisis, Beijing conducted unprecedented exercises and suspended senior military and maritime-security dialogue with Washington—the channels most useful for preventing miscalculation. China also vetoed additional UN sanctions after North Korea’s accelerating missile tests, while arguing that diplomacy and relief from pressure offered a better solution.

Important constraints included U.S.–China rivalry, U.S. and Russian much larger arsenals, missile defence concerns, Russia’s invasion of Ukraine, the Taiwan crisis, North Korean escalation and the stalled Iran talks. China’s closed information system makes absence of accidents or unauthorized-use incidents non-probative. Civil nuclear regulation is more observable than military custody, launch authority, fissile-material stocks or operational alerting.

Overall, the record is mixed but asymmetrical: formal restraint and risk-governance positions were substantial, while observable force expansion, opacity and interruption of crisis communications materially increased uncertainty and escalation risk. No score is assigned.

## Ten-question disposition

1. **1B.1 — Mixed, adverse force trend.** The P5 statement and NFU diplomacy sought risk reduction (WEB-1B-002, 005), but silo construction and arsenal growth were materially contrary evidence (WEB-1B-003, 004). Public evidence does not establish whether expansion was solely retaliatory survivability or also coercive leverage.

2. **1B.2 — Generally restrained explicit nuclear language, with limits.** China reaffirmed NFU, defensive purpose and the proposition that nuclear war cannot be won (WEB-1B-002, 005). No Xi-attributed 2022 explicit nuclear-use threat was located. This finding concerns known rhetoric only; silence is not proof of responsible operational planning.

3. **1B.3 — Favorable formal safeguards; military implementation opaque.** The P5 pledge addressed unintended and unauthorized use, China had detailed civil nuclear-safety law, and selected peaceful facilities remained under IAEA safeguards (WEB-1B-002, 010, 011). No independent target-year evidence establishes military warhead custody, personnel reliability, launch authentication or accident rates.

4. **1B.4 — Mixed.** China supported NPT, CTBT, safeguards, fissile-material negotiations and JCPOA restoration (WEB-1B-005, 008, 011), but did not accept arsenal limits or intrusive weapons transparency while expanding rapidly (WEB-1B-003, 012).

5. **1B.5 — Material adverse crisis evidence.** China’s arsenal was not openly invoked to threaten nuclear use, but strategic-deterrence expansion occurred alongside intensified Taiwan coercion; unprecedented exercises and severed communications increased escalation risk (WEB-1B-004, 006). Evidence does not prove that Xi explicitly used nuclear weapons as a shield for the exercises.

6. **1B.6 — Mixed to adverse.** China promoted restoration of constraints on Iran (WEB-1B-008), but vetoed new DPRK sanctions amid accelerating prohibited missile activity (WEB-1B-007). Beijing’s stated rationale—sanctions could intensify confrontation and diplomacy was preferable—is relevant limiting evidence, but the veto removed a concrete enforcement measure.

7. **1B.7 — Institutional investment visible, expert independence unclear.** Xi called for strengthened risk-monitoring, nuclear, cyber and biological security systems; China’s AI paper required risk assessment and accountability (WEB-1B-001, 009). No reliable evidence was found on whether nuclear decision-making privileged independent technical expertise over political loyalty.

8. **1B.8 — Mixed, with a major adverse crisis choice.** China advocated strategic-stability dialogue and JCPOA diplomacy (WEB-1B-002, 005, 008), but suspended senior military and maritime-safety communications during the acute Taiwan crisis (WEB-1B-006). Strategic-stability dialogue remained underdeveloped (WEB-1B-012).

9. **1B.9 — Favorable formal policy, weak transparency.** Military-AI policy endorsed human control, operator accountability, testing and restraint; Xi called for biological, cyber, data and nuclear risk systems; civil nuclear law imposed preventive and emergency duties (WEB-1B-001, 009, 010). Evidence of implementation, independent audits or disclosure of military cyber/biological programmes remains inadequate.

10. **1B.10 — More dangerous observable nuclear posture by year-end, despite formal restraints.** Expansion, opacity, entanglement and crisis-channel suspension increased capability and miscalculation risk (WEB-1B-003, 004, 006, 012). NFU, low-alert traditions and risk-governance commitments are genuine counterweights, but no verified reduction or binding constraint offset the growth.

## Source handling

**Independent corroboration**

- SIPRI’s target-year technical estimate corroborated satellite-observed silo expansion and the 350-warhead opening baseline.
- The U.S. DoD independently estimated more than 400 operational warheads by late 2022.
- Carnegie independently assessed the strategic consequences of silo growth, command-system entanglement and absent dialogue.
- Reuters independently documented the Taiwan exercises and cancelled communications.
- IAEA independently verified only safeguarded material at selected civil facilities—not China’s weapons stockpile.

**Reused supplied leads reopened and verified**

E001, E002, E034, E035, E036/IAEA component, E040, E042, E044, E046 and E048.

**Uninspected leads**

E003, E037, E038, E039, E041, E045 and E047 were not necessary to an accepted fact or could not be inspected within the available session. The local D17/FAS files could not be accessed because the workspace shell was unavailable.

**Rejected**

- Wikipedia P5 statement lead: replaced with the underlying Chinese MFA text.
- Official assertions that China maintained only a “minimum” deterrent: retained as declarations but rejected as independent proof of force size or implementation.
- Absence of reported nuclear accidents: rejected as evidence of safety.
- Civil nuclear law as proof of military-warhead custody: rejected; article 92 expressly leaves military nuclear safety to separate State Council/CMC rules.
- U.S. DoD projections to 2035: not treated as a 2022 outcome.

**Blocked/partial**

- IAEA Safeguards Implementation Report PDF: searchable official text was available, but the PDF open returned an internal error; classified as `partial_or_landing_page`, not fully extracted.
- UN press page for the DPRK sanctions vote timed out when reopened; its already-opened UN search text supports the procedural vote, but the machine record is conservatively marked `indexed_text_only`.
- The local researcher guide and local D17/FAS facts were inaccessible in this tool environment.

## Discovery and disposition counts

- Distinct source leads reviewed: **28**

- Reused supplied records: **10**
- Uninspected supplied leads: **7**
- Rejected source/claim uses: **5**
- Blocked opening attempts: **3**
- Accepted official Chinese sources: **6**
- Accepted independent/intergovernmental sources: **6**

## Remaining questions and saturation

Material gaps remain on nuclear-release procedure, warhead custody, alert status, personnel reliability, accident history, fissile-material production, internal expert deliberation, biological-program transparency and implementation of military-AI principles. More research in classified or newly released Chinese material could be decisive. Additional ordinary English-language web searching was beginning to repeat the same official declarations and external technical estimates; therefore the public-source dossier is provisionally saturated, but the military command-and-control and expert-governance lenses remain lower confidence.
